, and a polynomial otherwise. Because the global functional equation depends only on
d_{i}
, not
f_{i}
themselves, this sum satisfies the global functional equation (
4.9
). Note that, by Axiom
3
, the second sum is
qD(x,(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r-1}}))
for an arbitrary monic linear polynomial
\pi
.

We use Lemma
4.4
to write the second sum as

qD(x,(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r-1}}))=qD_{\pi}(x,(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r-1}}))D^{(\pi)}(x,\vec{1},\pi^{v}).

If
n\nmid v
, then
D^{(\pi)}(x,\vec{1},\pi^{v})
is the Dirichlet
L
-function
L(x,\left(\frac{\pi^{v}}{*}\right)_{\chi})
. This
L
-function with conductor
\pi
of degree
1
is simply equal to
1
. If
n|v
, then
D^{(\pi)}(x,\vec{1},\pi^{v})
is the function field zeta function with the Euler factor at
\pi
removed. This evaluates to
\frac{1-x}{1-qx}
.

If we set
b=1
if
n\mid v
and
b=0
otherwise, then the functional equation (
4.9
), multiplied by
(1-qx)^{b}q^{\frac{-A-1}{2}}
, gives the following:

\begin{split}&(1-qx)^{b}q^{\frac{-A-1}{2}}\sum_{\deg f_{1}=d_{1},\ldots\deg f_{r-1}=d_{r-1}}D(x,\vec{f};M)\\
&-(1-x)^{b}q^{\frac{-A+1}{2}}D_{\pi}(x,(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r-1}});M)\\
&=\frac{\omega g_{\chi^{v}}^{b-1}}{\chi(-1)^{v}x}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r-1\\
M_{ir}\neq 0\end{subarray}}(\chi(-1)^{v}xg_{\chi^{M_{ir}}})^{d_{i}}\left(x-1\right)^{b}q^{\frac{-A-1}{2}}\sum_{\deg f_{1}=d_{1},\ldots\deg f_{r-1}=d_{r-1}}D\left(\frac{1}{qx},\vec{f};M^{\prime}\right)\\
&\phantom{=}-\frac{\omega g_{\chi^{v}}^{b-1}}{\chi(-1)^{v}x}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r-1\\
M_{ir}\neq 0\end{subarray}}(\chi(-1)^{v}xg_{\chi^{M_{ir}}})^{d_{i}}\left(x-\frac{1}{q}\right)^{b}q^{\frac{-A+1}{2}}D_{\pi}\left(\frac{1}{qx},(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r-1}});M^{\prime}\right).\end{split}

(4.19)

Moreover, this is an equality of polynomials in
x
. Note that the same logic of the previous paragraph also applies to
qD(x,(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r-1}});M^{\prime})
, and the value of
v
for this series is congruent to the negative of the previous
v
value modulo
n
.

Recall that we call a power series in
x
“sharp” if each coefficient of
x^{j}
is a linear combination of
q
-Weil numbers of weights greater than
j
and “flat” if each coefficient of
x^{j}
is a linear combination of
q
-Weil numbers of weights less than
j
. By Axioms
4
and
5
, the terms in the first line of (
4.19
) are sharp, and the terms in the second line are flat. Since the first and second lines sum to a polynomial in
x
and there is no cancellation between them, we conclude that each one is a polynomial in
x
. Again by Axioms
4
and
5
, the terms in the third line of (
4.19
) are sharp, and the terms in the fourth line are flat. Thus we conclude that the first line equals the third line, and the second line equals the fourth line.

Because the second line of (
4.19
) is a polynomial,
(1-qx)^{b}D(x,(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r}});M)
is a polynomial, which verifies the global rationality statement when
\vec{f}=(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r-1}})
and
\deg\pi=1
, the last remaining case with
\deg f_{i}=d_{i}
. The equality of the second and fourth lines is equivalent to

\begin{split}&D(x,(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r-1}});M)=\frac{\omega g_{\chi^{v}}^{b-1}}{\chi(-1)^{v}x}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r-1\\
M_{ir}\neq 0\end{subarray}}(\chi(-1)^{v}xg_{\chi^{M_{ir}}})^{d_{i}}\left(\frac{x-1}{1-qx}\right)^{b}D\left(\frac{1}{qx},(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r-1}});M^{\prime}\right)\end{split}

which verifies the global functional equation (
4.9
) in this last remaining case.

The fact that the second line of (
4.19
) is a polynomial also verifies the local rationality statement for
\deg\pi=1
,
\vec{f}=(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r-1}})
. The equality of the second and fourth lines can be written with the local series as

\begin{split}&D_{\pi}(x,(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r-1}});M)\\
&=\omega_{\pi}\frac{(g_{\chi^{v}}q^{-1})^{b-1}}{\chi(-1)^{v}qx}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r-1\\
M_{ir}\neq 0\end{subarray}}(\chi(-1)^{v}xg_{\chi^{M_{ir}}})^{d_{i}}\left(\frac{qx-1}{1-x}\right)^{b}D_{\pi}\left(\frac{1}{qx},(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r-1}});M^{\prime}\right)\end{split}

where

\omega_{\pi}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r-1\\
M_{ir}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\xi(-1)^{d_{i}(d_{i}-1)/2}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq r-1\\
M_{ir},M_{jr}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\chi(-1)^{d_{i}d_{j}M_{ir}}=\pm 1.

This is the local functional equation when
\vec{f}=(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r-1}})
and
\deg\pi=1
.

To extend to
\pi
of higher degree, we apply Axiom
3
. The effect replacing
\pi
of degree
1
with
\pi
of arbitrary degree is to replace
x
by
x^{\deg\pi}
, take all
q
-Weil numbers to the power of
\deg\pi
(with the Gauss sum
g_{\chi}
understood as
-1
times the Weil number
-g_{\chi}
), divide the left by
\left(\frac{\pi^{\prime}}{\pi}\right)_{\chi}^{\sum d_{i}M_{ii}}
and the right by
\left(\frac{\pi^{\prime}}{\pi}\right)_{\chi}^{\sum d_{i}M_{ii}^{\prime}}
. Note that all these operations preserve rationality, so we verify the local rationality statement for arbitrary
\pi
. After performing these operations on the functional equation and simplifying, we obtain

\begin{split}&D_{\pi}(x,(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r-1}});M)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r-1\\
M_{ir}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{\pi^{\prime}}{\pi}\right)_{\chi}^{-d_{i}(M_{ir}+n/2)}\omega_{\pi}\left(\frac{-(-g_{\chi^{v}})^{\deg\pi}}{q^{\deg\pi}}\right)^{b_{\pi}-1}(\chi(-1)^{v}qx)^{-\deg\pi}\\
&\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r-1\\
M_{ir}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left((\chi(-1)^{v}qx)^{\deg\pi}\left(\frac{-(g_{\chi^{M_{ir}}})^{\deg\pi}}{q^{\deg\pi}}\right)\right)^{d_{i}}\left(\frac{(qx)^{\deg\pi}-1}{1-x^{\deg\pi}}\right)^{b_{\pi}}D_{\pi}\left(\frac{1}{qx},(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r-1}});M^{\prime}\right)\end{split}

with notation as in (
4.10
). This is the desired local functional equation for arbitrary
\pi
.
∎

4.2
Multivariable Functional Equation

As with the Kubota functional equations, we deduce functional equations for the multivariable series based on the single-variable functional equations. We again set
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\ldots x_{r})\in\mathbb{C}^{r}
,
\vec{k}=(k_{1},\ldots k_{r})\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}
and let

\displaystyle Z(\vec{x},\vec{k};q,\chi,M)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{1},\ldots f_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[T]^{+}\\
\deg f_{i}\equiv k_{i}\bmod n\end{subarray}}a(f_{1},\ldots f_{r};q,\chi,M)x_{1}^{\deg f_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{\deg f_{r}}

(4.20)

\displaystyle Z_{\pi}(\vec{x},\vec{k};q,\chi,M)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d_{1},\ldots d_{r}\geq 0\\
d_{i}\equiv k_{i}\bmod n\end{subarray}}a(\pi^{d_{1}},\ldots\pi^{d_{r}};q,\chi,M)x_{1}^{d_{1}\deg\pi}\cdots x_{r}^{d_{r}\deg\pi}.

(4.21)

We now introduce notation relevant for the multivariable functional equations. Fix
i\in\{1,\ldots r\}
, and assume that
M_{ii}=0
. Set
e_{ij}=1
if
M_{ij}\neq 0
,
e_{ij}=0
if
M_{ij}=0
. For fixed
k_{1},\ldots k_{i-1},k_{i+1},\ldots k_{r}
, let
v=k_{1}M_{1i}+\cdots+k_{r}M_{ri}
,
v_{i}=k_{i+1}M_{i\,(i+1)}+\cdots+k_{r}M_{ir}
,
K=k_{1}e_{1i}+\cdots+k_{r}e_{ri}
,
b=1
if
n|v
,
b=0
otherwise, and

\omega=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq r\\
M_{ji}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\xi(-1)^{k_{j}(k_{j}-1)/2}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq h<j\leq r\\
M_{hi},M_{ji}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\chi(-1)^{k_{h}k_{j}M_{hi}}.

Define a transformation
\sigma_{i;q,\chi,M}:\mathbb{C}^{r}\to\mathbb{C}^{r}
as follows:

(\sigma_{i;q,\chi,M}(\vec{x}))_{j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{qx_{i}}&j=i\\
(g_{\chi^{M_{ij}}}x_{i})^{e_{ij}}x_{j}&j\neq i\end{array}\right..

(4.22)

Define a transformation
\tau_{i}
on
r\times r
symmetric integer matrices as follows:

\begin{split}&(\tau_{i}(M))_{ii}=M_{ii}=0\\
&(\tau_{i}(M))_{ij}=-M_{ij}\text{ for all }j\neq i\\
&(\tau_{i}(M))_{jj}=M_{jj}+e_{ji}(M_{ji}+n/2)\text{ for all }j\neq i\\
&(\tau_{i}(M))_{hj}=M_{hj}+e_{hi}e_{ij}(M_{hi}+M_{ij})\text{ for all }h\neq j\neq i.\end{split}

(4.23)

Recall that
S^{k,n}
is defined as the operation which transforms a power series
D(x)
into the sum of all terms in
D(x)
whose powers of
x
are congruent to
k
mod
n
. Properties of this operation are listed before Theorem
3.10
.

We can now prove the multivariable global functional equation.

Theorem 4.7
.

The vector
(Z(\vec{x},(k_{1},\ldots k_{r});M)_{k_{i}}
satisfies a functional equation

\begin{split}Z(\vec{x},(k_{1},\ldots k_{r});M)_{k_{i}}=&\omega g_{\chi^{v}}^{b-1}\left(\chi^{(v+v_{i})(k_{i}+\ell_{i})}(-1)\Theta_{k_{i},\ell_{i}}(x_{i},K)\right)_{k_{i},\ell_{i}}\\
&Z(\sigma_{i}(\vec{x}),(k_{1},\ldots k_{i-1},\ell_{i},k_{i+1},\ldots k_{r});\tau_{i}(M))_{\ell_{i}}\end{split}

(4.24)

where the
n\times n
scattering matrix
(\Theta_{k,\ell}(x,K))_{k,\ell}
has entries as follows. For
n\nmid v
, we have
\Theta_{k,\ell}(x,K)=x^{-1}
if
k+\ell\equiv K-1\bmod n
, and
\Theta_{k,\ell}(x,K)=0
otherwise.
For
n\mid v
, we have

\Theta_{k,\ell}(x,K)=x^{-1}\left(\frac{q^{n-1}x^{n}-1}{1-q^{n}x^{n}}\right)

if
k+\ell\equiv K-1\bmod n
and

\Theta_{k,\ell}(x,K)=x^{-1}\left(\frac{(q^{-1}-1)(qx)^{\left(k+\ell-K+1\right)\%n}}{1-q^{n}x^{n}}\right)

otherwise. Here
\omega
,
b
,
v
,
v_{i}
, and
K
are defined as above; they depend on
M
,
i
and
k_{j}
for
j\neq i
.

This functional equation is equivalent to
[
33
, Theorem 6]
, though our method of proof is analytic rather than geometric.

Proof.

First we prove the result when
i=r
. We have

\begin{split}Z(\vec{x},(k_{1},\ldots k_{r});M)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{1},\ldots f_{r-1}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[T]^{+}\\
\deg f_{i}\equiv k_{i}\bmod n\end{subarray}}x_{1}^{\deg f_{1}}\cdots x_{r-1}^{\deg f_{r-1}}S^{k_{r},n}D(x_{r},(f_{1},\ldots f_{r-1});M).\end{split}

We can then apply the global functional equation of Theorem
4.6
and rearrange to obtain

\begin{split}Z(\vec{x},(k_{1},\ldots k_{r});M)=&\omega\,g_{\chi^{v}}^{b-1}\sum_{f_{1},\ldots f_{r-1}}\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}(g_{\chi^{M_{ir}}}^{e_{ir}}x_{r}^{e_{ir}}x_{i})^{\deg f_{i}}x_{r}^{-1}\chi^{v(K-1)}(-1)\\
&\cdot S^{k_{r}-K+1,n}\left(\frac{x_{r}-1}{1-qx_{r}}\right)^{b}D\left(\frac{1}{qx_{r}},(f_{1},\ldots f_{r-1});\tau_{r}(M)\right).\end{split}

Suppose that
n\nmid v
, so
b=0
. We find that

Z(\vec{x},(k_{1},\ldots k_{r});M)=\omega\,g_{\chi^{v}}^{-1}\chi^{v(K-1)}(-1)x_{r}^{-1}Z(\sigma_{r}(\vec{x_{r}}),(k_{1},\ldots k_{r-1},K-1-k_{r});\tau_{r}(M))

which is the desired result since
\Theta_{k,\ell}(x_{r},K)=x_{r}^{-1}
if
k+\ell\equiv K-1\bmod n
, and
\Theta_{k,\ell}(x_{r},K)=0
otherwise.

Now suppose that
n|v
, so
b=1
. This expression for
Z(\vec{x},(k_{1},\ldots k_{r});M)
becomes

\begin{split}&\omega\sum_{f_{1},\ldots f_{r-1}}\prod_{i=1}^{r-1}(g_{\chi^{M_{ir}}}^{e_{ir}}x_{r}^{e_{ir}}x_{i})^{\deg f_{i}}x_{r}^{-1}\sum_{\ell_{r}=0}^{n-1}S^{k_{r}+\ell_{r}-K+1,n}\left(\frac{x_{r}-1}{1-qx_{r}}\right)S^{-\ell_{r}}D\left(\frac{1}{qx_{r}},(f_{1},\ldots f_{r-1});\tau_{r}(M)\right)\\
&=\omega x_{r}^{-1}\sum_{\ell_{r}=0}^{n-1}S^{k_{r}+\ell_{r}-K+1,n}\left(\frac{x_{r}-1}{1-qx_{r}}\right)Z(\sigma_{r}(\vec{x_{r}}),(k_{1},\ldots k_{r-1},\ell_{r});\tau_{r}(M))\end{split}

which is the desired result since
\Theta_{k,\ell}(x_{r},K)=x_{r}^{-1}S^{k_{r}+\ell_{r}-K+1,n}\left(\frac{x_{r}-1}{1-qx_{r}}\right)
.

This proves the theorem for
i=r
. The extension of the proof to arbitrary
i
is exactly the same as in the proof of Theorem
3.10
; reordering the variables simply introduces an additional
\chi^{v_{i}(k_{i}+\ell_{i})}(-1)
into the entries of the scattering matrix.
∎

We also have the analogous local functional equation.

Theorem 4.8
.

The vector
(Z_{\pi}(\vec{x},(k_{1},\ldots k_{r});M)_{k_{i}}
satisfies a functional equation

\begin{split}Z_{\pi}(\vec{x},(k_{1},\ldots k_{r});M)_{k_{i}}=&\omega^{\deg\pi}\left(\frac{-(-g_{\chi^{v}})^{\deg\pi}}{q^{\deg\pi}}\right)^{b-1}(-1)^{(\deg\pi+1)K}\left(\frac{\pi^{\prime}}{\pi}\right)_{\chi}^{-v-Kn/2}\\
&(\chi^{(v+v_{i})(k_{i}+\ell_{i})\deg\pi}(-1)\Theta_{\pi,k_{i},\ell_{i}}(x_{i},K))_{k_{i},\ell_{i}}\\
&Z_{\pi}(\sigma_{i}(\vec{x}),(k_{1},\ldots k_{i-1},\ell_{i},k_{i+1},\ldots k_{r});\tau_{i}(M))_{\ell_{i}}\end{split}

(4.25)

where the
n\times n
scattering matrix
(\Theta_{k,\ell}(x,K))_{k,\ell}
has entries as follows. For
n\nmid v
, we have
\Theta_{\pi,k,\ell}(x,K)=(qx)^{-\deg\pi}
if
k+\ell\equiv K-1\bmod n
, and
\Theta_{\pi,k,\ell}(x,K)=0
otherwise.
For
n\mid v
, we have

\Theta_{\pi,k,\ell}(x,K)=(qx)^{-\deg\pi}\left(\frac{q^{\deg\pi}x^{n\deg\pi}-1}{1-x^{n\deg\pi}}\right)

if
k+\ell\equiv K-1\bmod n
and

\Theta_{\pi,k,\ell}(x,K)=(qx)^{-\deg\pi}\left(\frac{(q^{\deg\pi}-1)x^{\deg\pi\left(k+\ell-K+1\right)\%n}}{1-x^{n\deg\pi}}\right)

otherwise. Here
\omega
,
b
,
v
,
v_{i}
, and
K
are defined as above; they depend on
M
,
i
and
k_{j}
for
j\neq i
.

The proof is the same as that of Theorem
4.7
, using the local functional equation in Theorem
4.6
.

4.3
When the Functional Equations Agree

The functional equations in Theorems
3.10
and
4.7
(or Theorems
3.11
and
4.8
in the local setting) appear to be quite different from each other. But there is a special situation where their hypotheses overlap, and in this case we can check that the functional equations match. We assume that
q\equiv 1\bmod 4
, that
M_{ii}=0
, and that
n/2|M_{ij}
for all
j
. This is the only possible case where the hypotheses on the matrix
M
used in this section and the previous section overlap. This hypothesis will imply that the single-variable subseries in
x_{i}
behave like quadratic Dirichlet
L
-functions, or Kubota
L
-series involving quadratic Gauss sums. Because of Gauss’s evaluation of the quadratic Gauss sums (
4.2
), the Dirichlet and Kubota series match up to a change of variables, so they satisfy equivalent functional equations.

In this case, we have
n_{i}=2
, and
e_{ij}=n_{ij}=1
if
M_{ij}=n/2
and
e_{ij}=n_{ij}=0
if
M_{ij}=0
so the definitions of
F
and
K
used in Sections
3
and
4
agree. The transformations
\sigma_{i}
underlying the functional equation given in (
3.23
) and (
4.22
) agree:

\sigma_{i}(\vec{x})_{j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{qx_{i}}&j=i\\
x_{j}\left(\frac{qx_{i}}{g_{\xi}}\right)^{n_{ij}}&j\neq i\end{array}\right..

This is straightforward to verify since we have
g_{\xi}^{2}=q
in this case. It is also straightforward to check that
\tau_{i}(M)=M
.

Finally, we check the equality of the global scattering matrices:

\left(\chi^{v_{i}(k+\ell)}(-1)E_{n_{i}}^{n}\Gamma_{k,\ell}(x,K)\right)_{k,\ell}=\omega g_{\chi^{v}}^{b-1}\left(\chi^{(v+v_{i})(k+\ell)}(-1)\Theta_{k,\ell}(x,K)\right)_{k,\ell}.

Using the fact that each
M_{ij}
is
n/2
or
0
, and using
q\equiv 1\bmod 4
, it follows that
\chi^{v_{i}(k+\ell)}=\chi^{(v+v_{i})(k+\ell)}(-1)=\omega=1
. So it suffices to check that
E_{2}^{n}\Gamma_{k,\ell}(x,K)=g_{\chi^{v}}^{b-1}\Theta_{k,\ell}(x,K)
.

Note that
v=0
,
b=1
if
K
is even and
v=n/2
,
b=0
if
K
is odd. If
K
is even,

(\Gamma_{k,\ell}(x,K))_{k,\ell}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1-q}{1-q^{2}x^{2}}&\frac{qx-x^{-1}}{1-q^{2}x^{2}}\\
\frac{qx-x^{-1}}{1-q^{2}x^{2}}&\frac{1-q}{1-q^{2}x^{2}}\end{pmatrix}

so
E_{2}^{n}\Gamma_{k,\ell}(x_{i},K)=S^{k+\ell-K,n}\left(\frac{qx-x^{-1}}{1-q^{2}x^{2}}\right)
for
k+\ell
odd, and
E_{2}^{n}\Gamma_{k,\ell}(x_{i},K)=S^{k+\ell-K,n}\left(\frac{1-q}{1-q^{2}x^{2}}\right)
for
k+\ell
even. This matches

\Theta_{k,\ell}(x,K)=x^{-1}S^{k+\ell-K+1,n}\left(\frac{x-1}{1-qx}\right)=S^{k+\ell-K,n}\left(\frac{1-x^{-1}}{1-qx}\right)=S^{k+\ell-K,n}\left(\frac{1-q+qx-x^{-1}}{1-q^{2}x^{2}}\right).

If
K
is odd,

(\Gamma_{k,\ell}(x,K))_{k,\ell}=\begin{pmatrix}\left(\frac{qx}{g_{\xi}}\right)^{-1}&0\\
0&\left(\frac{qx}{g_{\xi}}\right)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

so
E_{2}^{n}\Gamma_{k,\ell}(x_{i},K)=\left(\frac{qx}{g_{\xi}}\right)^{-1}
if
k+\ell\equiv K-1\bmod n
,
E_{2}^{n}\Gamma_{k,\ell}(x_{i},K)=0
otherwise. This matches the definition of
\omega g_{\xi}^{-1}\Theta_{k,\ell}(x,K)
in this case.

We have verified that the global functional equations of Theorems
3.10
and
4.7
match in the overlapping case. The local functional equations can be checked similarly. All these formulas also agree with the functional equations of
[
47
]
.

5
Groupoid Structure

5.1
Arithmetic root systems and multiple Dirichlet series

For Weyl group multiple Dirichlet series, the functional equations form a group, the Weyl group of a finite root system, which acts on
\mathbb{C}^{r}
. The functional equation corresponding to a group element can be expressed as a linear relationship between the value of the series at a point and the value at the image of that point under the group element. More generally, one must consider linear relations between the values of a tuple of series at a point and the values at its image under a group element, with the exact linear relations expressed using a scattering matrix.

While the functional equation of Kubota type fits into this framework, the functional equation of Dirichlet type does not, since it sometimes relates the values of a multiple Dirichlet series to the values of an apparently totally different multiple Dirichlet series. Instead, the functional equations form the algebraic structure of a groupoid – we have finitely many different series, each a holomorphic function on
\mathbb{C}^{r}
, and functional equations relating a pair of series give isomorphisms between the corresponding copies of
\mathbb{C}^{r}
, relating the values of the first series at a point and the second series at its image under the isomorphism.

The relevant groupoids were constructed, in a different context, by István Heckenberger, as the Weyl groupoids of arithmetic root systems, forming part of the study of Nichols algebras of diagonal type. In the next section we will explain the connection between multiple Dirichlet series and Nichols algebras and explore the relationship between the functional equations and the Weyl groupoid, but for now we only need to explain the notion of an arithmetic root system at a formal level.

In a specialization of
[
36
, §2]
, let
\psi\colon\mathbb{Z}^{r}\times\mathbb{Z}^{r}\to\mathbb{\mu}_{\infty}
be a bicharacter where
\mu_{\infty}
is the group of roots of unity of
\mathbb{C}
, i.e.
\psi(a+b,c)=\psi(a,c)\psi(b,c)
and
\psi(a,b+c)=\psi(a,b)\psi(a,c)
for all
a,b,c\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}
. (In fact, one can check that the only part of
\psi
relevant for the construction is its restriction to the diagonal
\psi(a,a)
, but the full bicharacter is relevant for its original application.)

Let
E=(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})
be an ordered basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
. We say
E
is admissible at
i
if
\psi(e_{i},e_{i})\neq 1
and
E
is admissible if it is admissible at
i
for all
i
. For each
j\in\{1,\dots,r\}
with
j\neq i
, let
m_{ij}=\min(m\in\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}\mid\psi(e_{i},e_{i})^{m+1}=1\textrm{ or }\psi(e_{i},e_{i})^{m}\psi(e_{i},e_{j})\psi(e_{j},e_{i})=1\}
. For
E
admissible at
i
, let
s_{i,E}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{r}\to\mathbb{Z}^{r}
be the unique map with
s_{i,E}(e_{i})=-e_{i}
and
s_{i,E}(e_{j})=e_{j}+m_{ij}e_{i}
if
j\neq i
.

For
E
any fixed ordered basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
, let
B_{\psi,E}
be the smallest set of ordered bases of
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
such that
E\in B_{h\chi,E}
and for any
E^{\prime}\in B_{\psi,E}
if
E^{\prime}
is admissible at
i
then
s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})\in B_{\psi,E}
.

We now discuss the Weyl groupoid of a multiple Dirichlet series, following
[
37
, p. 262, third paragraph]
and not the original definition of Heckenberger. First define an equivalence relation on
B_{\psi,E}
where
(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})
and
(e_{1}^{\prime},\dots,e_{r}^{\prime})
are equivalent if and only if
\psi(e_{i}^{\prime},e_{i}^{\prime})=\psi(e_{i},e_{i})
for all
i
and
\psi(e_{i}^{\prime},e_{j}^{\prime})\psi(e_{j}^{\prime},e_{i}^{\prime})=\psi(e_{i},e_{j})\psi(e_{j},e_{i})
for all
i,j
. The objects of Weyl groupoids are the equivalence classes for this equivalence relation. The morphisms from the equivalence class of
E^{\prime}=(e_{1}^{\prime},\dots,e_{r}^{\prime})
to the equivalence class of
E^{\prime\prime}
are the set of
r\times r
matrices
N
such that
(\sum_{j=1}^{n}N_{1j}e_{j}^{\prime},\dots,\sum_{j=1}^{n}N_{1j}e_{j}^{\prime})
is equivalent to
E^{\prime\prime}
. This manifestly depends only on the equivalence class of
E^{\prime\prime}
but less obviously depends only on the equivalence class of
E^{\prime}
. Composition of morphisms is given by matrix composition.

Let us consider an illustrative example.

Example 5.1
.

For
\mathfrak{g}
a simple Lie algebra, we can identify
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
with the lattice of roots, which admits an integer-valued pairing
\langle,\rangle
. Fixing a root of unity
v
and letting
\mathbf{\mathsf{q}}=v^{2}
, we can set
\psi(a,b)=v^{\langle a,b\rangle}
. Let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
be the simple roots of
\mathfrak{g}
and let
E
be the basis
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})
. We have
\psi(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})=\mathbf{\mathsf{q}}^{\frac{\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle}{2}}
and

\psi(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})\psi(\alpha_{j},\alpha_{i})=\mathbf{\mathsf{q}}^{\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}\rangle}

so that

\psi(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})^{m}\psi(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})\psi(\alpha_{j},\alpha_{i})=1

for
m=-2\frac{\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}\rangle}{\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle}
. If
\mathbf{\mathsf{q}}
avoids some finite list of roots of unity depending on
\mathfrak{g}
, we will not have
\psi(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})^{\ell}=1
for any
\ell\leq m
so that in fact
m_{ij}=-2\frac{\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}\rangle}{\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle}
. Then
s_{i,E}
is the reflection around the root
\alpha_{i}
in the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}
. Using this, we see that
B_{\psi,E}
is the set of bases of the root lattice given by all possible sets of simple roots.

All bases in
B_{\psi,E}
are equivalent, so the Weyl groupoid has one object, and the isotropy group of that object is the Weyl group.

The variable
\mathbf{\mathsf{q}}
is denoted by
q
in
[
1
,
35
]
. To avoid ambiguity with
q
the cardinality of a finite field, or, later,
q
in the sense of quantum groups, we use a different font for
\mathbf{\mathsf{q}}
.

Thus
B_{\psi,E}
is a generalization of the set of all possible collections of simple roots in the root lattice of a simple Lie algebra.

If
B_{\psi,E}
is finite and all bases in
B_{\psi,E}
are admissible, then
[
36
, Definition 1]
sets
\mathbf{\Delta}=\bigcup_{E\in B_{\psi,E}}E
and defines
(\mathbf{\Delta},\psi,E)
to be an arithmetic root system.

The relationship of this to multiple Dirichlet series is as follows. For a bicharacter
\psi
, fix a root of unity
\zeta_{1}
of even order such that every value taken by
\psi
is a power of
\zeta_{1}
. Then define
M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}}_{ij}
to be the smallest nonnegative integer
m
with
\zeta_{1}^{m}=\psi(e_{i},e_{j})\psi(e_{j},e_{i})
if
i\neq j
or the smallest nonnegative integer
m
with
\zeta_{1}^{m}=-\psi(e_{i},e_{i})
if
i=j
. The basic theorem relating the functional equations to the reflections
s_{i,E}
of the Weyl groupoid is as follows.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\psi\colon\mathbb{Z}^{r}\times\mathbb{Z}^{r}\to\mu_{\infty}
be a bicharacter,
E
a basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
, and
\zeta_{1}
a root of unity of even order
n
such that every value taken by
\psi
is a power of
\zeta_{1}
. Fix
i\in\{1,\dots,r\}
and assume
E
is admissible at
i
. Fix a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and a character
\chi\colon\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
of order
n
.

1.

If
\psi(e_{i},a)\psi(a,e_{i})
is a power of
\psi(e_{i},e_{i})
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}
and
q\equiv 1\bmod 4
, then the exponent of
x_{i}
in each monomial with nonvanishing coefficient in
\left(1-q^{n/n_{i}}\left(\frac{qx_{i}}{g}\right)^{n}\right)Z(\vec{x},(k_{1},\ldots k_{r});q,\chi,M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}})
is bounded in terms of the other exponents and we have the functional equation

(Z(\vec{x},(k_{1},\ldots k_{r});q,\chi,M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}}))_{k_{i}}

=\left(\chi^{v_{i}(k_{i}+\ell_{i})}(-1)E_{n_{i}}^{n}\Gamma_{k_{i},\ell_{i}}(x_{i},K)\right)_{k_{i},\ell_{i}}(Z(\sigma_{i}(\vec{x}),(k_{1},\ldots,k_{i-1},\ell_{i},k_{i+1},\ldots,k_{r});q,\chi,M^{\psi,s_{i,E}(E),\zeta_{1}}))_{\ell_{i}}

with
K,v_{i},\sigma_{i},\Gamma
and
E_{n_{i}}^{n}
defined as in §
3.2
.

2.

If
\psi(e_{i},e_{i})=-1
then the exponent of
x_{i}
in each monomial with nonvanishing coefficient in
\left(1-q^{n}x_{i}^{n}\right)^{n}Z(\vec{x},(k_{1},\ldots k_{r});q,\chi,M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}}
is bounded in terms of the other exponents and we have the functional equation

(Z(\vec{x},(k_{1},\ldots k_{r});q,\chi,M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}}))_{k_{i}}

=\omega g_{\chi^{v}}^{b-1}\left(\chi^{(v+v_{i})(k_{i}+\ell_{i})}(-1)\Theta_{k_{i},\ell_{i}}(x_{i},K)\right)_{k_{i},\ell_{i}}(Z(\sigma_{i}(\vec{x}),(k_{1},\ldots,k_{i-1},\ell_{i},k_{i+1},\ldots,k_{r});q,\chi,M^{\psi,s_{i,E}(E),\zeta_{1}}))_{\ell_{i}}

with
v,v_{i},K,b,\omega,\sigma_{i}
, and
\Theta_{k,\ell}
defined as in §
4.2
.

Moreover, in either case we have a relationship between
\sigma_{i}
and
s_{i,E}
. Consider the unique bilinear pairing
\langle,\rangle_{E}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{r}\times(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{r}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\langle e_{j},\vec{x}\rangle=\log(\sqrt{q}|x_{j}|)
for all
j\in E
.

Then for all
\vec{x}\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{r}
and
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}
we have

\langle s_{i,E}(a),\vec{x}\rangle_{E}=\langle a,\sigma_{i}(\vec{x})\rangle_{E}.

(5.1)

Proof.

In case (1), the hypothesis that
\psi(e_{i},e_{j})\psi(e_{j},e_{i})
is a power of
\psi(e_{i},e_{i})
implies that
M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}}_{ij}
is an integer multiple of
M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}}_{ii}+\frac{n}{2}
for all
j
. This is the hypothesis of the functional equation proved in Theorem
3.10
. The difference between the stated equation and the equation in Theorem
3.10
is that Theorem
3.10
relates
Z(\vec{x},(k_{1},\ldots k_{r});q,\chi,M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}})
to itself rather than to the apparently different series
Z(\vec{x},(k_{1},\ldots k_{r});q,\chi,M^{\psi,s_{i}(E),\zeta_{1}})
. However, we will see in Lemma
5.16
(2) that in fact
M^{\psi,s_{i}(E),\zeta_{1}}=M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}}
so these two series are the same. The boundedness of exponents follows from the rationality property of Theorem
3.9
since multiplying a rational function by its denominator produces a polynomial and the exponents of monomials appearing in a polynomial are bounded.

In case (2) the hypothesis that
\psi(e_{i},e_{i})=-1
implies that
M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}}_{ii}=0
, which is the standing assumption made in §
4.2
, allowing us to apply Theorem
4.7
. The difference between the stated equation and the equation of Theorem
4.7
is that Theorem
4.7
has
\tau_{i}(M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}})
instead of
M^{\psi,s_{i,E}(E),\zeta_{1}}
. So we must check these two matrices are equal, which is done in Lemma
5.17
(2) below. The boundedness of exponents follows from the rationality property of Theorem
4.6
.

To check (
5.1
), it suffices to handle the case
a=e_{j}
since the
e_{i}
form a basis for
\mathbb{Z}_{r}
. We handle each case in turn.

If
i\neq j
, in case (1) then we will see in Lemma
5.16
(1) that
m_{ij}=n_{ij}
. Using this, we have

\langle e_{j},\sigma_{i}(\vec{x})\rangle_{E}=\log\Bigl(\sqrt{q}\Bigl|x_{j}\Bigl(\frac{qx_{i}}{g}\Bigr)^{n_{ij}}\Bigr|\Bigr)=\log(\sqrt{q}|x_{j}|(\sqrt{q}|x_{i}|)^{n_{ij}})=\log(\sqrt{q}|x_{j}|(\sqrt{q}|x_{i}|)^{m_{ij}})

Similarly, in case (2), we will see in Lemma
5.17
(2) that
m_{ij}=e_{ij}
. Using this, we have

\langle e_{j},\sigma_{i}(\vec{x})\rangle_{E}=\log\Bigl(\sqrt{q}\Bigl|x_{j}\Bigl((g_{\chi^{M_{ij}}}x_{i}\Bigr)^{e_{ij}}\Bigr|\Bigr)=\log(\sqrt{q}|x_{j}|(\sqrt{q}|x_{i}|)^{e_{ij}})=\log(\sqrt{q}|x_{j}|(\sqrt{q}|x_{i}|)^{m_{ij}})

In either case we therefore have

\langle e_{j},\sigma_{i}(\vec{x})\rangle_{E}=\log(\sqrt{q}|x_{j}|(\sqrt{q}|x_{i}|)^{m_{ij}})=\langle e_{j},\vec{x}\rangle_{E}+m_{ij}\langle e_{i},\vec{x}\rangle_{E}=\langle e_{j}+m_{ij}e_{i},\vec{x}\rangle_{E}=\langle s_{i,E}(e_{j}),\vec{x}\rangle_{E}

as desired.

For
j=i
, in case (1) we have

\langle e_{j},\sigma_{i}(\vec{x})\rangle_{E}=\log\Bigl(\sqrt{q}\Bigl|\frac{g^{2}}{q^{2}x_{i}}\Bigr|\Bigr)=\log\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}|x_{i}|}

and in case (2) we have

\langle e_{j},\sigma_{i}(\vec{x})\rangle_{E}=\log\Bigl(\sqrt{q}\Bigl|\frac{1}{qx_{i}}\Bigr|\Bigr)=\log\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}|x_{i}|}

so in either case we have

\langle e_{j},\sigma_{i}(\vec{x})\rangle_{E}=\log\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}|x_{i}|}=-\langle e_{i},\vec{x}\rangle_{E}=\langle-e_{i},\vec{x}\rangle_{E}=\langle s_{i,E}(e_{i}),\vec{x}\rangle_{E}.

This completes the verification of (
5.1
).∎

Two bases
E
and
E^{\prime}
are equivalent in the equivalence relation defining the Weyl groupoid if and only if
M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}}=M^{\psi,E^{\prime},\zeta_{1}}
, so the objects of the Weyl groupoid correspond to choices of matrices
M
and therefore to multiple Dirichlet series. It follows that if
(\mathbf{\Delta},\psi,E)
is an arithmetic root system such that each
e\in\mathbf{\Delta}
satisfies either condition (1) or (2) of Theorem
5.2
then each morphism in the Weyl groupoid from one series to another corresponds to a combination of functional equations that we may use to relate the two Dirichlet series. In particular, the multiple Dirichlet series
Z(s_{1},\dots,s_{r};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}})
has a finite groupoid of functional equations, relating finitely many different series, but such that each of the series has a functional equation to itself which inverts each of the variables. This turns out to be the crucial condition to obtain desired properties like meromorphic continuation.

Here we can take advantage of the classification of arithmetic root systems proven in
[
35
]
, though we find the organization of the classification in
[
1
]
enlightening. The following theorem shows the arithmetic root systems to which Theorem
5.2
applies, and thus to which our subsequent results Theorem
5.5
and
5.6
will apply.

Theorem 5.3
.

Every root of the following arithmetic root systems, in the notation of
[
1
]
, satisfies either condition (1) or (2) of Theorem
5.2
:

•

All those of Cartan type (i.e. agreeing with the root system of a simple Le algebra over
\mathbb{C}
):
\mathtt{A}_{\theta},\mathtt{B}_{\theta},\mathtt{C}_{\theta},\mathtt{D}_{\theta},\mathtt{E}_{\theta},\mathtt{F}_{4},
and
\mathtt{G}_{2}

•

All those of super type (i.e. agreeing with the root system of a simple super Lie algebra over
\mathbb{C}
):
\mathtt{A}(j\mid\theta-j),\mathtt{B}(j\mid\theta-j),\mathtt{C}(j\mid\theta-j),\mathtt{D}(j\mid\theta-j),\mathtt{D}(2,1;\alpha),\mathtt{F}(4),
and
\mathtt{G}(3)

•

\mathtt{wk}(4)
, agreeing with the root system of a simple Lie algebra over a field of characteristic
2

•

\mathtt{g}(1,6)
when its parameter
\zeta
is a third root of unity and not a sixth root of unity,
\mathtt{g}(2,3),

\mathtt{g}(3,3),

\mathtt{g}(4,3),\mathtt{g}(3,6),

\mathtt{g}(2,6),

\mathtt{el}(5,3),

\mathtt{g}(8,3),

\mathtt{g}(4,6),

\mathtt{g}(6,6),
and
\mathtt{g}(8,6)
, all agreeing with the root system of simple super Lie algebras over fields of characteristic
3

•

\mathtt{brj}(2,5)
and
\mathtt{el}(5;5)
, both agreeing with the root systems of simple super Lie algebras over fields of characteristic
5

•

\mathtt{ufo}(1),\mathtt{ufo}(2),\mathtt{ufo}(6),
and
\mathtt{ufo}(12)
, called UFOs because they do not correspond in any known way to Lie algebras

Proof.

This can be checked by examining the generalized Dynkin diagrams provided for each arithmetic root system in
[
1
]
. The generalized Dynkin diagram associated to a pair
(\psi,E)
has one node for each
i
from
1
to
r
. Each node is labeled by
\psi(e_{i},e_{i})
. Two nodes are connected by an edge if
\psi(e_{i},e_{j})\psi(e_{j},e_{i})\neq 1
and, in this case, the edge is labeled by
\psi(e_{i},e_{j})\psi(e_{j},e_{i})
. Thus, to check that each root satisfies (1) or (2), it suffices to examine the Dynkin diagrams and check that each node is either labeled by
-1
or else connects only to edges with labels an integer power of its vertex label. This is easy to do.

The relationship of the root systems to Lie algebras are also taken from
[
1
]
.
∎

The multiple Dirichlet series arising from these arithmetic root systems include many of those appearing previously in the literature, or at least
\mathbb{F}_{q}(T)
-analogues of them.

We will see shortly in Example
5.11
that the multiple Dirichlet series arising from arithmetic root systems of Cartan type give the Weyl group multiple Dirichlet series. The Weyl group multiple Dirichlet series are known to have a direct relationship to the arithmetic of the algebraic groups with the same root system, as Whittaker coefficients of Eisenstein series on metaplectic covers of those groups
[
40
,
42
,
11
]
. It would be interesting to give a similar uniform description of the multiple Dirichlet series arising from arithmetic root systems of super type involving the corresponding super Lie algebra.

For the multiple Dirichlet series arising from arithmetic root systems of Lie algebras and super Lie algebras over fields of characteristic
2,3,
and
5
, a challenge is that these multiple Dirichlet series fail to be defined exactly over fields
\mathbb{F}_{q}
of characteristic matching the Lie algebra characteristic, since their definition requires multiplicative characters of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
of order a power of the Lie algebra characteristic. So one cannot consider the Lie algebra as a Lie algebra over
\mathbb{F}_{q}(T)
in the same way that the metaplectic Eisenstein series construction requires considering algebraic groups over the relevant global field.

To deduce properties like meromorphic continuation from the functional equations in a uniform way, one needs the following result of Heckenberger.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
(\mathbf{\Delta},\psi,E)
be an arithmetic root system. For each basis
E
of
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
, let
C_{E}
be the cone in
\mathbb{R}^{r}
consisting of vectors whose dot product with each vector in
E
is nonnegative. Then the interiors of the
C_{E^{\prime}}
are disjoint from each other and

\bigcup_{E^{\prime}\in B_{\psi,E}}C_{E^{\prime}}=\mathbb{R}^{r}.

In the classical root system case, the
C_{E^{\prime}}
are the Weyl chambers, and the assertion that the union of the Weyl chambers fill the space is standard. The key step in the proof of the lemma is that cones related by a simple reflection share a face, which may also be familiar in the Weyl chamber case.

Proof.

[
36
, Proposition 2]
states that
\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{r}\mid v\cdot e\neq 0\textrm{ for all }e\in\mathbf{\Delta}\}
is the disjoint union of the sets
\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{r}\mid v\cdot e_{i}>0\textrm{ for all }i\}
, i.e. the interiors of the
C_{E^{\prime}}
. Since the
C_{E^{\prime}}
are the closures of their interiors, and there are finitely many of them, their union is the closure of the union of their interiors. Since
\mathbf{\Delta}
is finite, the closure of
\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{r}\mid v\cdot e\neq 0\textrm{ for all }e\in\mathbf{\Delta}\}
is
\mathbb{R}^{r}
.∎

We are now ready to state the a result on the meromorphic continuation of multiple Dirichlet series, or, more precisely, the rationality of these series.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
(\mathbf{\Delta},\psi,E)
be an arithmetic root system such that each
i\in\mathbf{\Delta}
satisfies either condition (1) or (2) of Theorem
5.2
. Let
\zeta_{1}
be a root of unity such that every value taken by
\psi
is a power of
\zeta_{1}
. Fix a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with
q\equiv 1\bmod 4
and a character
\chi\colon\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
of order the order of
\zeta_{1}
. Then for all
E^{\prime}\in B_{\psi,E}
, the function
Z(\vec{x};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E^{\prime},\zeta_{1}})
is in fact a rational function of the variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{r}
.

Typically in the multiple Dirichlet series literature one proves that a multiple Dirichlet series is meromorphic by proving holomorphicity on some domain, using the functional equations to extend meromorphicity to a larger domain, finally use convexity to obtain meromorphicity on the whole space. In the function field case, using growth estimates, one can conclude that the function is rational. The starting point, holomorphicity on some domain, is not at all obvious for axiomatic multiple Dirichlet series but was established by Hase-Liu
[
33
, Theorem 1]
using geometric means. However, to make the argument work one must in addition show meromorphic continuation to a domain which overlaps with its translate under the functional equation, so that the functional equation gives an equality of meromorphic functions on the intersection reason. Hase-Liu checks this for functional equations of Dirichlet type but not of Kubota type. The additional verification is possible but we do not take this approach here.

Instead, we use an argument that is only possible in the function field setting, where we directly show rationality by manipulating a formal power series. This argument does not use any analysis, but morally has the same structure as the complex-analytic argument.

Proof.

For
v\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
a vector, we say that a formal power series
Z(\vec{x})
is
v,E^{\prime}
-bounded if there exists a polynomial
F
in
x_{1},\dots,x_{r}
such that the set of sums
\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}v\cdot e_{i}^{\prime}
such that
\prod_{i=1}^{r}x_{i}^{d_{i}}
appears with nonzero coefficient in
F(\vec{x})Z(\vec{x})
is bounded below.

For
v\in C_{E^{\prime}}
so that
v\cdot e_{i}^{\prime}\geq 0
for all
i
, the power series
Z(\vec{x};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E^{\prime},\zeta_{1}})
is automatically
v,E^{\prime}
-bounded, taking
F=1
, since only monomials of nonnegative degrees appear in it.

For
v
in the interior of
-C_{E^{\prime}}
, so that
v\cdot e_{i}^{\prime}<0
for all
i
, if
Z(\vec{x};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E^{\prime},\zeta_{1}})
is
v,E^{\prime}
-bounded then
F(\vec{x})Z(\vec{x};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E^{\prime},\zeta_{1}})
is a polynomial and hence
Z(\vec{x};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E^{\prime},\zeta_{1}})
is a rational function.

We can always take
v
in the interior of
-C_{E^{\prime}}
and then, by Proposition
5.4
,
v
will lie in
C_{E^{*}}
for some
E^{*}\in B_{\psi,E}
. By the previous two observations, it suffices to prove that if
Z(\vec{x};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E^{*},\zeta_{1}})
is
v,E^{*}
-bounded for one
E^{*}\in B_{\psi,E}
then
Z(\vec{x};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E^{\prime},\zeta_{1}})
is
v,E^{\prime}
-bounded for all
E^{\prime}\in B_{\psi,E}
. Since, by definition of
B_{\psi,E}
,
E^{\prime}
is mapped to
E^{*}
by a finite composition of simple reflections, it suffices by induction to prove that if
Z(\vec{x};q,\chi,M^{\psi,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime}),\zeta_{1}})
is
v,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})
-bounded then
Z(\vec{x};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E^{\prime},\zeta_{1}})
is
v,E^{\prime}
-bounded.

If
Z(\vec{x};q,\chi,M^{\psi,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime}),\zeta_{1}})
is
v,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})
-bounded then, replacing
F(\vec{x})
by
\prod_{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{r}\in\langle\zeta_{1}\rangle}F(\gamma_{1}x_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{r}x_{r})
, the same is true for
Z(\vec{x},\vec{k};q,\chi,M^{\psi,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime}),\zeta_{1}})
for all
\vec{k}
.

Now we check that
Z(\sigma_{i}(\vec{x}),\vec{k};q,\chi,M^{\psi,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime}),\zeta_{1}})
is
(v,E^{\prime})
-bounded. For integers
d_{1},\dots,d_{r}
, the composition of the monomial
\prod_{j=1}^{d_{r}}(x_{j})
with
\sigma_{i}
is a scalar multiple of the monomial

x_{i}^{-d_{i}}\prod_{j\neq i}(x_{j}x_{i}^{n_{ji}})^{d_{j}}=x_{i}^{-d_{i}+\sum_{j\neq i}n_{ji}}\prod_{j\neq i}x_{j}^{d_{j}}.

Since the map on the set of monomials that sends
\prod_{j=1}^{d_{r}}(x_{j})
to
x_{i}^{-d_{i}+\sum_{j\neq i}n_{ji}}\prod_{j\neq i}x_{j}^{d_{j}}
is a bijection, the monomial
\prod_{j=1}^{r}x_{j}^{d_{j}}
has nonzero coefficient in
F(\vec{x})Z(\vec{x},\vec{k};q,\chi,M^{\psi,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime}),\zeta_{1}})
if and only if the monomial
x_{i}^{-d_{i}+\sum_{j\neq i}n_{ji}d_{j}}\prod_{i\neq j}x_{j}^{d_{j}}
has nonzero coefficient in
F(\sigma_{i}(\vec{x}))Z(\sigma_{i}(\vec{x}),\vec{k};q,\chi,M^{\psi,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime}),\zeta_{1}})
. For the set of tuples
d_{1},\dots,d_{r}
satisfying either of these two equivalent conditions, we have

(-d_{i}+\sum_{j\neq i}n_{ji}d_{j})v\cdot e_{i}^{\prime}+\sum_{j\neq i}d_{j}v\cdot e_{j^{\prime}}=d_{i}v\cdot(-e_{i}^{\prime})+\sum_{j\neq i}d_{j}v\cdot(e_{j^{\prime}}+m_{ij}e_{i^{\prime}})=\sum_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}v\cdot s_{i,E^{\prime}}(e_{i}^{\prime})

and the quantities
\sum_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}v\cdot s_{i,E^{\prime}}(e_{i}^{\prime})
are bounded below since
Z(\vec{x},\vec{k};q,\chi,M^{\psi,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime}),\zeta_{1}})
is
v,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})
-bounded, hence the quantities
(-d_{i}+\sum_{j\neq i}n_{ji}d_{j})v\cdot e_{i}^{\prime}+\sum_{j\neq i}d_{j}v\cdot e_{j^{\prime}}
are bounded below and thus
Z(\sigma_{i}(\vec{x}),\vec{k};q,\chi,M^{\psi,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime}),\zeta_{1}})
is
(v,E^{\prime})
-bounded.

It is straightforward to check that multiplication by a rational function and summation both preserve the property of being
v,E^{\prime}
-bounded. (For the rational function case, we multiply
F
by the denominator of the rational function. This is the only step in the argument for which the inclusion of
F
in the definition of
v,E^{\prime}
-bounded is necessary.)

Because multiplication by a rational function and summation preserve the property of being
v,E^{\prime}
-bounded, it follows from the functional equation of Theorem
5.2
that
Z(\vec{x},\vec{k};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E^{\prime},\zeta_{1}})
is
v,E^{\prime}
-bounded. Summing over
\vec{k}
, we see that
Z(\vec{x};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E^{\prime},\zeta_{1}})
is
v,E^{\prime}
-bounded, completing the induction step.

(We could have simplified the proof of the induction step by making the induction hypothesis that
Z(\vec{x},\vec{k};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E^{\prime},\zeta_{1}})
is
(v,E^{\prime})
-bounded for all
\vec{k}
, at the cost of complicating the explanation of the structure of the induction argument.) ∎

Now we state the theorem that our multiple Dirichlet series are uniquely determined by their functional equations.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
(\mathbf{\Delta},\psi,E)
be an arithmetic root system such that each root in
\mathbf{\Delta}
satisfies either condition (1) or (2) of Theorem
5.2
. Let
\zeta_{1}
be a root of unity of even order
n
such that every value taken by
\psi
is a power of
\zeta_{1}
. Fix a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with
q\equiv 1\bmod 4
and a character
\chi\colon\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
of order
n
.

There exists a unique collection of formal power series
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};E^{\prime})
for each
E^{\prime}\in B_{\psi,E}
and
\vec{k}\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}^{r}
, satisfying all the following conditions:

1.

For each
\vec{k},E^{\prime}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})
, and
i\in\{1,\dots,r\}
such that
\psi(e_{i},a)\psi(a,e_{i})
is a power of
\psi(e_{i},e_{i})
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}
, the exponent of
x_{i}
in each monomial appearing with nonvanishing coefficient in
\left(1-q\left(\frac{qx_{i}}{g}\right)^{n}\right)\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};E^{\prime})
(where
g=g_{\xi\chi^{M_{ii}^{\psi,E^{\prime},\zeta_{1}}}}
) is bounded in terms of the other exponents and we have the functional equation

(\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},(k_{1},\ldots k_{r});E^{\prime})_{k_{i}}

=\left(\chi^{v_{i}(k_{i}+\ell_{i})}(-1)E_{n_{i}}^{n}\Gamma_{k_{i},\ell_{i}}(x_{i},K)\right)_{k_{i},\ell_{i}}(\widetilde{Z}(\sigma_{i}(\vec{x}),(k_{1},\ldots,k_{i-1},\ell_{i},k_{i+1},\ldots,k_{r});s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})))_{\ell_{i}}

2.

For each
\vec{k},E^{\prime}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})
, and
i\in\{1,\dots,r\}
such that
\psi(e_{i},e_{i})=-1
, the exponent of
x_{i}
in each monomial appearing with nonvanishing coefficient in
\left(1-q^{n}x_{i}^{n}\right)\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};E^{\prime})
is bounded in terms of the other exponents and we have the functional equation

(\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},(k_{1},\ldots k_{r});E^{\prime})_{k_{i}}

=\omega g_{\chi^{v}}^{b-1}\left(\chi^{(v+v_{i})(k_{i}+\ell_{i})}(-1)\Theta_{k_{i},\ell_{i}}(x_{i},K)\right)_{k_{i},\ell_{i}}(\widetilde{Z}(\sigma_{i}(\vec{x}),(k_{1},\ldots,k_{i-1},\ell_{i},k_{i+1},\ldots,k_{r});s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})))_{\ell_{i}}

3.

Z(\vec{x},(0,\dots,0),E)
has constant term
1
.

Those power series are given by

\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};E^{\prime})=Z(\vec{x},\vec{k};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E^{\prime},\zeta_{1}}).

(5.2)

Before proving Theorem
5.6
, we prove some lemmas that involve a single functional equation relating a pair of multiple Dirichlet series. Lemmas
5.7
and
5.8
will involve a single-variable functional equation while Lemma
5.9
, proven using the previous two, will involve a multivariable functional equation. In Lemmas
5.7
and Lemmas
5.8
we will not state the single-variable functional equations as we did when we originally proved them, but rather in a form that is easier to derive from the multivariable functional equations.

We could add to Theorem
5.6
the condition that the exponent of
x_{i}
in each monomial with non-vanishing coefficient in
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};E^{\prime})
is congruent to
k_{i}
mod
n
, a property that is satisfied, by definition, by
Z(\vec{x},\vec{k};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E^{\prime},\zeta_{1}})
. This would simplify the proof, but only slightly, so we prefer to avoid imposing this condition.

Lemma 5.7
.

Fix a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and a character
\chi\colon\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
of even order
n
. Let
v,v_{i},
and
K
be integers and
\omega\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Set
b=1
if
n\mid v
and
b=0
otherwise. Let
\widetilde{d}
be an integer. Let
(S(x,k))_{k}
and
(S^{\prime}(x,k))_{k}
be
n
-dimensional vectors of power series in
x
such that
(1-q^{n}x^{n})S(x,k)
and
(1-q^{n}x^{n})S^{\prime}(x,k)
are polynomials in
x
. Suppose that

(S(x,k))_{k}=x^{\widetilde{d}}\omega g_{\chi^{v}}^{b-1}\left(\chi^{(v+v_{i})(k+\ell)}(-1)\Theta_{k,\ell}(x,K)\right)_{k,\ell}\left(S^{\prime}(\frac{1}{qx},\ell)\right)_{\ell}.

(5.3)

Let
d^{\mathrm{min}}
be the minimum integer
d
such that the coefficient of
x^{d}
in
S(x,k)
is nonzero for at least one
k
. Let
d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}
be the minimum integer
d
such that the coefficient of
x^{d}
in
S^{\prime}(x,k)
is nonzero for at least one
k
.
If
d^{\mathrm{min}}<\infty
then

d^{\mathrm{min}}+d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}\leq\widetilde{d}.

(5.4)

Proof.

Since
d^{\mathrm{min}}<\infty
,
S(x,k)
is nonzero for at least one
k
, so by the invertibility of the Fourier transform there is
\eta\in\mu_{n}
such that

\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\eta^{k}S(x,k)\neq 0.

(5.5)

The functional equation (
5.3
) together with the formula for
\Theta_{k,\ell}(x,K)
gives

\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\eta^{k}S(x,k)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}S^{\prime}(\frac{1}{qx},\ell)\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\eta^{k}x^{\widetilde{d}}\omega g_{\chi^{v}}^{b-1}\chi^{(v+v_{i})(k+\ell)}(-1)\Theta_{k,\ell}(x,K)

=\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\eta^{-\ell}S^{\prime}(\frac{1}{qx},\ell)x^{\widetilde{d}}\omega\times\begin{cases}\eta^{K-1}g_{\chi^{v}}^{-1}\chi^{(v+v_{i})(K-1)}(-1)x^{-1}&\mathrm{if}n\nmid v\\
\eta^{K-1}\chi^{(v+v_{i})(K-1)}(-1)x^{-1}\frac{1-\eta\chi^{v+v_{i}}(-1)x}{1-\eta\chi^{v+v_{i}}(-1)qx}&\mathrm{if}n\mid v\end{cases}.

Let
\eta^{\prime}=\eta\chi^{v+v_{i}}(-1)
.

Now we know that
(1-q^{n}x^{n})\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\eta^{-\ell}S^{\prime}(x,\ell)
is a polynomial in
x
. Hence
(1-x^{-n})\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\eta^{-\ell}S^{\prime}(\frac{1}{qx},\ell)
is a polynomial in
x^{-1}
. Since we obtain
\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\eta^{k}S(x,k)
by multiplying by a polynomial in
x,x^{-1}
and possibly dividing by
(1-\eta^{\prime}qx)
, we conclude that
(1-x^{-n})(1-\eta^{\prime}qx)\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\eta^{k}S(x,k)
is a polynomial in
x,x^{-1}
. On the other hand, we also know that
(1-q^{n}x^{n})\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\eta^{k}S(x,k)
is a polynomial in
x,x^{-1}
. It follows that
(1-\eta^{\prime}qx)\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\eta^{k}S(x,k)
is a polynomial in
x,x^{-1}
, where
(1-\eta^{\prime}qx)
is the greatest common divisor of
(1-x^{-n})(1-\eta^{\prime}qx)
and
(1-q^{n}x^{n})
in the ring
\mathbb{C}[x,x^{-1}]
.

Hence we obtain an identity of polynomials in
x,x^{-1}
:

(1-\eta^{\prime}qx)\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\eta^{k}S(x,k)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\eta^{-\ell}S^{\prime}(\frac{1}{qx},\ell)x^{\widetilde{d}}\omega\times\begin{cases}\eta^{K-1}g_{\chi^{v}}^{-1}\chi^{(v+v_{i})(K-1)}(-1)x^{-1}(1-\eta^{\prime}qx)&\textrm{if }n\nmid v\\
\eta^{K-1}\chi^{(v+v_{i})(K-1)}(-1)x^{-1}(1-\eta^{\prime}x)&\textrm{if }n\mid v\end{cases}.

Since the coefficient of
x^{d}
in
S^{\prime}(x,\ell)
is zero for
d<d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}
, the coefficient of
x^{d}
in
\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\eta^{-\ell}S^{\prime}(\frac{1}{qx},\ell)
is zero for
d>-d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}
and thus the coefficient of
x^{d}
in
\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\eta^{-\ell}S^{\prime}(\frac{1}{qx},\ell)x^{\widetilde{d}}
is zero for
d>\widetilde{d}-d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}
. Since both
x^{-1}(1-\eta^{\prime}x)
and
x^{-1}(1-\eta^{\prime}qx)
have degree
\leq 0
in
x
, the coefficient of
x^{d}
in
(1-\eta^{\prime}qx)\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\eta^{k}S(x,k)
is zero for
d>\widetilde{d}-d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}
.

However, (
5.5
) implies that the coefficient of
d
in
(1-\eta^{\prime}qx)\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\eta^{k}S(x,k)
is nonzero for some
d
. By assumption, we have
d\geq d^{\mathrm{min}}
, and we have just seen that
d\leq\widetilde{d}-d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}
. Combining these, we obtain (
5.4
).
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Fix a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and a character
\chi\colon\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
of even order
n
. Let
n_{i}
be a divisor of
n
and
M_{ii}
an integer such that
\gcd(M_{ii}+\frac{n}{2},n)=\frac{n}{n_{i}}
. Let
K
and
v
be integers. Let
c\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and let
\widetilde{d}
be an integer. Let
(S(x,k))_{k}
and
(S^{\prime}(x,k))_{k}
be
n
-dimensional vectors of power series in
x
such that
\left(1-q^{n/n_{i}}\left(\frac{qx}{g}\right)^{n}\right)S(x,k)
and
(1-q^{n/n_{i}}\left(\frac{qx}{g}\right)^{n})(S^{\prime}(x,k))
(where
g=g_{\xi\chi^{M_{i}i}}
) are polynomials in
x
. Suppose that

(S(x,k))_{k}=cx^{\widetilde{d}}\left(\chi^{v(k+\ell)}(-1)E_{n_{i}}^{n}\Gamma_{k,\ell}(x,K)\right)_{k,\ell}\Bigl(S^{\prime}(\frac{g^{2}}{q^{2}x},\ell)\Bigr)_{\ell}.

(5.6)

Let
d^{\mathrm{min}}
be the minimum integer
d
such that the coefficient of
x^{d}
in
S(x,k)
is nonzero for at least one
k
. Let
d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}
be the minimum integer
d
such that the coefficient of
x^{d}
in
S^{\prime}(x,k)
is nonzero for at least one
k
.
If
d^{\mathrm{min}}<\infty
then

d^{\mathrm{min}}+d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}\leq\widetilde{d}.

(5.7)

Proof.

Since
d^{\mathrm{min}}<\infty
,
S(x,k)
is nonzero for at least one
k
, so by the invertibility of the Fourier transform there is
\eta\in\mu_{n}
and
\kappa\in\{0,\dots,n_{i}-1\}
such that

\sum_{k\equiv\kappa\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{k}S(x,k)\neq 0.

(5.8)

The functional equation (
5.6
) together with the definition of
E_{n_{i}}^{n}
gives

\begin{split}\left(\sum_{k\equiv\kappa\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{k}S(x,k)\right)_{\kappa}&=cx^{\widetilde{d}}\Bigl(\sum_{\lambda=0}^{n_{i}-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\equiv\kappa\bmod n_{i}\\
\ell\equiv\lambda\bmod n_{i}\end{subarray}}\eta^{k}\chi^{v(k+\ell)}(-1)S^{k+\ell-K,n}\Gamma_{\kappa,\lambda}(x,K)S^{\prime}(\frac{g^{2}}{q^{2}x},\ell)\Bigr)_{\kappa}\\
&=cx^{\widetilde{d}}\eta^{{}^{\prime}K}x^{\widetilde{d}}\left(\Gamma_{\kappa,\lambda}(\eta^{\prime}x,K)\right)_{\kappa,\lambda}\Bigl(\sum_{\ell\equiv\lambda\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{-\ell}S^{\prime}(\frac{g^{2}}{q^{2}x},\ell)\Bigr)_{\lambda}\end{split}

where
\kappa
and
\lambda
range from
0
to
n_{i}-1
and
\eta^{\prime}=\chi^{v}(-1)\eta
is also an
n
th root of unity. For each
\lambda
, we know
(1-q^{n/n_{i}}\left(\frac{qx}{g}\right)^{n})\sum_{\ell\equiv\lambda\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{-\ell}S^{\prime}(x,\ell)
is a polynomial in
x
, so
(1-q^{n/n_{i}}\left(\frac{g}{qx}\right)^{n})\sum_{\ell\equiv\lambda\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{-\ell}S^{\prime}(\frac{g^{2}}{q^{2}x},\ell)
is a polynomial in
x^{-1}
. Since
\sum_{k\equiv\kappa\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{k}S(x,k)
is obtained from these values by applying a matrix whose entries are polynomials in
x,x^{-1}
divided by
1-q\left(\frac{\eta^{\prime}qx}{g}\right)^{n_{i}}
, we can conclude that
(1-q^{n/n_{i}}\left(\frac{g}{qx}\right)^{n})(1-q\left(\frac{\eta^{\prime}qx}{g}\right)^{n_{i}})\sum_{k\equiv\kappa\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{k}S(x,k)
is a polynomial in
x,x^{-1}
. On the other hand, we also know that
(1-q^{n/n_{i}}\left(\frac{qx}{g}\right)^{n})\sum_{k\equiv\kappa\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{k}S(x,k)
is a polynomial in
x
. Therefore, it follows that
(1-q\left(\frac{\eta^{\prime}qx}{g}\right)^{n_{i}})\sum_{k\equiv\kappa\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{k}S(x,k)
is a polynomial in
x,x^{-1}
, where
(1-q\left(\frac{\eta^{\prime}qx}{g}\right)^{n_{i}})
is the greatest common divisor of
(1-q^{n/n_{i}}\left(\frac{g}{qx}\right)^{n})(1-q\left(\frac{\eta^{\prime}qx}{g}\right)^{n_{i}})
and
(1-q\left(\frac{qx}{g}\right)^{n_{i}})
in the ring
\mathbb{C}[x,x^{-1}]
.

Thus we obtain an identity of vectors of polynomials

\left(1-q\left(\frac{\eta^{\prime}qx}{g}\right)^{n_{i}}\right)\Bigl(\sum_{k\equiv\kappa\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{k}S(x,k)\Bigr)_{\kappa}

=\left(1-q\left(\frac{\eta^{\prime}qx}{g}\right)^{n_{i}}\right)cx^{\widetilde{d}}\eta^{{}^{\prime}K}(\Gamma_{\kappa,\lambda}(\eta^{\prime}x,K))_{\kappa,\lambda}\Bigl(\sum_{\ell\equiv\lambda\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{-\ell}S^{\prime}(\frac{g^{2}}{q^{2}x},\ell)\Bigr)_{\lambda}.

We know the coefficient of
x^{d}
in
S^{\prime}(x,\ell)
vanishes for
d<d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}
. Hence the coefficient of
x^{d}
in
S^{\prime}(\frac{g^{2}}{q^{2}x},\ell)
vanishes for
d>-d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}
. After multiplying by
1-q\left(\frac{\eta^{\prime}qx}{g}\right)^{n_{i}}
, the entries of the scattering matrix
\Gamma(\eta^{\prime}x,K)
are polynomials in
x,x^{-1}
of degree
\leq 1
in
x
. Combined with the
x^{\widetilde{d}}
factor, it follows that the coefficient of
x^{d}
in
\left(1-q\left(\frac{\eta^{\prime}qx}{g}\right)^{n_{i}}\right)\sum_{k\equiv\kappa\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{k}S(x,k)
vanishes for
d>\widetilde{d}+1-d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}
. If (
5.7
) fails so that
\widetilde{d}-d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}<d^{\mathrm{min}}
, we conclude that the coefficient of
x^{d}
in
\left(1-q\left(\frac{\eta^{\prime}qx}{g}\right)^{n_{i}}\right)\sum_{k\equiv\kappa\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{k}S(x,k)
vanishes for
d\geq d^{\mathrm{min}}+1
. However, we already know that the coefficient of
x^{d}
vanishes for
d<d^{\mathrm{min}}
. Hence this coefficient is nonzero only for
d=d^{\mathrm{min}}
, and by (
5.8
) must in fact be nonzero for this
d
.

We now use the inverse matrix to express
\sum_{\ell\equiv\lambda\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{-\ell}S^{\prime}(x,\ell)
in terms of
\sum_{k\equiv\kappa\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{k}S(x,k)
. By Lemma
3.3
, the inverse matrix
(\Gamma(\eta^{\prime}x,K))^{-1}
is
\Gamma(\frac{g^{2}}{q^{2}\eta^{\prime}x},K)
. This implies

\Bigl(\sum_{\ell\equiv\lambda\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{-\ell}S^{\prime}(\frac{g^{2}}{q^{2}x},\ell)\Bigr)_{\lambda}=c^{-1}x^{-\widetilde{d}}(\eta^{\prime})^{-K}\left(\Gamma_{\lambda,\kappa}(\frac{g^{2}}{q^{2}\eta^{\prime}x},K)\right)_{\lambda,\kappa}\left(\sum_{k\equiv\kappa\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{k}S(x,k)\right)_{\kappa}.

which after the substitution
x\mapsto\frac{g^{2}}{q^{2}x}
gives

\Bigl(\sum_{\ell\equiv\lambda\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{-\ell}S^{\prime}(x,\ell)\Bigr)_{\lambda}=c^{-1}\left(\frac{q^{2}}{g^{2}}\right)^{\widetilde{d}}x^{\widetilde{d}}(\eta^{\prime})^{-K}\left(\Gamma_{\lambda,\kappa}((\eta^{\prime})^{-1}x,K)\right)_{\lambda,\kappa}\left(\sum_{k\equiv\kappa\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{k}S(\frac{q^{2}}{g^{2}x},k)\right)_{\kappa}.

Using that the coefficient of
x^{d}
in
\left(1-q\left(\frac{\eta^{\prime}qx}{g}\right)^{n_{i}}\right)\sum_{\ell\equiv\lambda\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{-\ell}S(x,\ell)
is nonzero only for
d=d^{\mathrm{min}}
, we see that the coefficient of
x^{d}
in
\left(1-q\left(\frac{g}{q(\eta^{\prime})^{-1}x}\right)^{n_{i}}\right)\sum_{\ell\equiv\lambda\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{-\ell}S(\frac{g^{2}}{q^{2}x},\ell)
is nonzero only for
d=-d^{\mathrm{min}}
(and is in fact nonzero for this
d
for some
\lambda
).

By assumption,
\left(\left(1-q^{n/n_{i}}\left(\frac{\eta^{\prime}qx}{g}\right)^{n}\right)\sum_{k\equiv\kappa\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{-k}S^{\prime}(x,k)\right)_{\kappa}
is a vector of polynomials in
x
.

By the functional equation and the above analysis of
\left(1-q\left(\frac{\eta^{\prime}qx}{g}\right)^{n_{i}}\right)\sum_{\ell\equiv\lambda\bmod n_{i}}\eta^{-\ell}S(x,\ell)
, this vector is a nontrivial
\mathbb{C}
-linear combination of the columns of
\Gamma((\eta^{\prime})^{-1}x,K)
times

x^{\widetilde{d}-d^{\mathrm{min}}}\frac{1-q^{n/n_{i}}\left(\frac{qx}{g}\right)^{n}}{1-q\left(\frac{g}{q(\eta^{\prime})^{-1}x}\right)^{n_{i}}}.

To check this is impossible, it suffices to check that a nontrivial
\mathbb{C}
-linear combination of the columns of
\Gamma((\eta^{\prime})^{-1}x,K)
is nontrivial mod
1-q\left(\frac{g}{q(\eta^{\prime})^{-1}x}\right)^{n_{i}}
, as then multiplying by a polynomial relatively prime to
1-q\left(\frac{g}{(\eta^{\prime})^{-1}qx}\right)^{n_{i}}
and then dividing by
1-q\left(\frac{g}{(\eta^{\prime})^{-1}qx}\right)^{n_{i}}
will always produce a non-polynomial rational function.

The
\kappa,\lambda
’th entry of
\Gamma(x,K)
is a sum of powers of
x
congruent to
k+\ell-K
modulo
n_{i}
, which implies different columns of the scattering matrix will not cancel, even modulo
1-q\left(\frac{g}{(\eta^{\prime})^{-1}qx_{i}}\right)^{n_{i}}
, so it suffices to check that each column of the scattering matrix is nonzero modulo
1-q\left(\frac{g}{(\eta^{\prime})^{-1}qx_{i}}\right)^{n_{i}}
, which follows from the explicit descriptions of, for example, the diagonal entries of
\Gamma((\eta^{\prime})^{-1}x,K)
, which are all nonzero modulo
1-q\left(\frac{g}{(\eta^{\prime})^{-1}qx_{i}}\right)^{n_{i}}
.∎

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
E=(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})
be a basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
,
\psi\colon\mathbb{Z}^{r}\times\mathbb{Z}^{r}\to\mu_{\infty}
a bicharacter, and
i\in\{1,\dots,r\}
such that
e_{i}
satisfies either condition (1) or (2) of Theorem
5.2
. Let
\zeta_{1}
be a root of unity of even order
n
such that every value taken by
\psi
is a power of
\zeta_{1}
. Fix a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and a character
\chi\colon\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
of order
n
.

Let
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};E)
and
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};s_{i,E}(E))
be two tuples of formal power series indexed by
\vec{k}\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}^{r}
satisfying the functional equation stated in Theorem
5.6
.

Let
d_{1},\dots,d_{i-1},d_{i+1},\dots,d_{r}
be a tuple of nonnegative integers. Let
d^{\mathrm{min}}
be the minimum integer
d
such that the coefficients of
x_{i}^{d}\prod_{j\neq i}x_{j}^{d_{j}}
in
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};E)
and
Z(\vec{x},\vec{k},q,\chi,M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}})
differ for at least one
\vec{k}
. Let
d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}
be the minimum integer
d^{\prime}
such that the coefficients of of
x_{i}^{d^{\prime}}\prod_{j\neq i}x_{j}^{d_{j}}
in
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};s_{i}(E))
and
Z(\vec{x},\vec{k},q,\chi,M^{\psi,s_{i,E}(E),\zeta_{1}})
differ for at least one
\vec{k}
.

If
d^{\mathrm{min}}<\infty
then

d^{\mathrm{min}}+d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}\leq\sum_{j\neq i}d_{j}m_{ij}.

(5.9)

Lemma
5.9
implies two weaker statements that we will also use. The first is that if
d^{\mathrm{min}}<\infty
then
d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}<\infty
. The second is that if
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};s_{i,E}(E))=Z(\vec{x},\vec{k};q,\chi,M^{\psi,s_{i}(E),\zeta_{1}})
then
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};E)=Z(\vec{x},\vec{k};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}})
. Both of these have shorter proofs, but we will prove them all together as the strongest statement is needed.

Proof.

After subtracting
Z(\vec{x},\vec{k},q,\chi,M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}})
from
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};E)
, and the same for
s_{i,E}(E)
, since the functional equation is linear and is known to hold for the multiple Dirichlet series
Z
, the functional equation still holds for the new values of
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};E)
and
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};s_{i,E}(E))
. Furthermore
d^{\mathrm{min}}
becomes the minimum integer
d
such that the coefficient of
x_{j}^{d}\prod_{j\neq i}x_{j}^{d_{j}}
in
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};s_{i,E}(E))
is nonzero for at least one
\vec{k}
and
d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}
becomes the minimum integer
d^{\prime}
such that the coefficient of
x_{j}^{d^{\prime}}\prod_{j\neq i}x_{j}^{d_{j}}
in
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};s_{i,E}(E))
is nonzero for at least one
\vec{k}
.

We will work with this definition of
d^{\mathrm{min}}
and
d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}
and show (
5.9
).

To do this, we fix
k_{1},\dots,k_{i-1},k_{i+1},\dots,k_{r}
such that the coefficient of
x_{j}^{d_{\mathrm{min}}}\prod_{j\neq i}x_{j}^{d_{j}}
in
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};s_{i,E}(E))
is nonzero for at least one
k_{i}
. We view

\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},k_{1},\dots,k_{i-1},k,k_{i+1},\dots,k_{r};E)

as a power series in
x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{r}
with coefficients power series of
x_{i}
and set
S(x_{i},k)
to be the coefficient of
\prod_{j\neq i}x_{j}^{d_{j}}
in

\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},k_{1},\dots,k_{i-1},k,k_{i+1},\dots,k_{r};E).

Similarly, we set
S^{\prime}(x_{i},k)
to be the coefficient of
\prod_{j\neq i}x_{j}^{d_{j}}
in

\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},k_{1},\dots,k_{i-1},k,k_{i+1},\dots,k_{r};s_{i,E}(E)).

We check that
S
and
S^{\prime}
satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
5.8
in case (1) and satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
5.7
in case (2), taking
\widetilde{d}=\sum_{j\neq i}d_{j}m_{ij}
. Having done this, (
5.7
) or (
5.4
) respectively will imply (
5.9
), since restricting attention to the fixed values
k_{1},\dots,k_{i-1},k_{i+1},\dots,k_{r}
preserves
d^{\mathrm{min}}
and can only increase
d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}
.

The boundedness of terms with nonvanishing coefficients assumption in the functional equation exactly implies the polynomiality assumption in Lemma
5.8
or
5.7
. To verify the functional equation itself, a key is that
\sigma_{i}
sends
x_{i}
to
\frac{g^{2}}{q^{2}x_{i}}
in case (1), matching the functional equation in Lemma
5.8
, and
\sigma_{i}
sends
x_{i}
to
\frac{1}{qx_{i}}
in case (2), matching the functional equation in Lemma
5.7
. Furthermore,
\sigma_{i}
multiplies each
x_{j}
variable for
j\neq i
by
x_{i}^{m_{ij}}
times a constant. The
x_{i}^{m_{ij}}
terms give a multiplicative factor of
x_{i}^{\sum_{j\neq i}d_{j}m_{ij}}=x_{i}^{\widetilde{d}}
while the constant may be absorbed into
c
in Lemma
5.8
or
\omega
in Lemma
5.7
. So we indeed obtain the functional equations from these lemmas. ∎

Proof of Proposition
5.6
.

The fact that
Z(\vec{x},\vec{k};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}})
satisfy these equations is the content of Theorem
5.2
, so we need only prove uniqueness. In other words, we fix a tuple of formal power series
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};E)
, assume the functional equations, and must prove (
5.2
).

A natural strategy of proof, previously used in the multiple Dirichlet series literature, is an induction on the degree of monomials: We check that if the coefficients of all monomials of total degree
<d
in the two sides of (
5.2
) agree then the same is true for monomials of total degree
\leq d
. For a variant, we can consider a weighted total degree where different variables are assigned different weights. The induction could be equivalently viewed as an argument by contradiction where we examine the monomial whose coefficients in the two sides of (
5.2
) differ of the least total degree and derive a contradiction.

Our proof is philosophically similar but more indirect: Rather than considering the least total degree we define a function on
\mathbb{R}^{r}
whose value at each point can be interpreted as the minimum of a weighted total degree. Examining the minimizers at each point, we will derive a contradiction by checking that this function cannot be concave and also must be concave.

Assume for contradiction that (
5.2
) is false for some
E^{\prime}\in B_{\psi,E},\vec{k}\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}^{r}
. It is straightforward to see that for each
E^{\prime}\in B_{\psi,E}
, there is some
\vec{k}\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}^{r}
for which (
5.2
) fails, since by Lemma
5.9
, if (
5.2
) fails for
s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E)
for some
\vec{k}
, then it also fails for
E^{\prime}
, for some
\vec{k}
.

For each
E^{\prime}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})\in B_{\psi,E}
, we define a function
F_{E^{\prime}}
on
C_{E^{\prime}}
: For
v\in C_{E^{\prime}}
, define
F_{E^{\prime}}(v)
to be the minimum value of
\sum_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}v\cdot e_{j}
over the set of pairs of
\vec{k}\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}^{r}
,
\vec{d}\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}
such that the coefficients of
\prod_{i=1}^{r}x_{i}^{d_{i}}
in
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};E^{\prime})
and
Z(\vec{x},\vec{k};q,\chi,M^{\psi,E^{\prime},\zeta_{1}})
are not equal.

There are at most finitely many values of
\vec{d}
that can contribute to the minimization in the definition of
F_{E^{\prime}}
, since every infinite subset of
\mathbb{N}^{r}
contains two different elements, one of which has all entries greater than or equal to the corresponding entries of the other. Because (
5.2
) fails for each
E^{\prime}
, the set minimized over is not empty. Hence
F_{E^{\prime}}
is a well-defined piecewise linear function, and, in particular, is continuous. Since the minimum of an arbitrary set of linear functions is concave,
F_{E^{\prime}}
is a concave function.

We will show that there is a single continuous function
F
on
\mathbb{R}^{r}
whose restriction to
C_{E^{\prime}}
is
F_{E^{\prime}}
for each
E^{\prime}
. We will show
F
cannot be concave and then derive a contradiction from this by showing that
F
must be concave.

Fix two adjacent cones
C_{E^{\prime}}
and
C_{s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})}
. Let us check that
F_{E^{\prime}}
and
F_{s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})}
agree on the intersection of
C_{E^{\prime}}
and
C_{s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})}
. Fix a point
v
in the boundary. Assume the minimum in the definition of
F_{E^{\prime}}(v)
is obtained by some pair
\vec{k},\vec{d}
. Then it follows from Lemma
5.9
that the coefficients of some monomial
\prod_{j=1}^{r}x_{j}^{d_{j}^{\prime}}
in
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k}^{\prime};s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime}))
and
Z(\vec{x},\vec{k}^{\prime};q,\chi,M^{\psi,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime}),\zeta_{1}})
are different, with
d_{j}^{\prime}=d_{j}
for all
j\neq i
(because
d^{\mathrm{min}}<\infty
and so
d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}<\infty
). Then since
v
lies on the boundary,
v\cdot e_{i}=0
and thus
v\cdot e_{j}=v\cdot s_{i,E^{\prime}}(e_{j})
for all
j
. Hence

F_{s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})}(v)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}^{\prime}v\cdot e_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}v\cdot e_{j}=F_{E^{\prime}}(v).

A symmetrical argument gives
F_{E^{\prime}}(v)\leq F_{s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})}(v)
and hence
F_{E^{\prime}}(v)=F_{s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})}(v)
.

Now consider two cones
C_{E^{\prime}}
and
C_{E^{*}}
that intersect. Again we check that
F_{E^{\prime}}
and
F_{E^{*}}
agree on the intersection of
C_{E^{\prime}}
and
C_{E^{*}}
. To do this, observe that their interesction is a lower-dimensional face. Since a neighborhood of that face is connected, we can find a sequence of cones, each adjacent to the next, starting with
C_{E^{\prime}}
and ending with
C_{E^{*}}
that all contain that face. Since each function agrees with the next on that face,
F_{E^{\prime}}
and
F_{E^{*}}
agree as well.

Since each
v\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
is in
C_{E^{\prime}}
for some
E^{\prime}
by Proposition
5.4
, and the value
F_{E^{\prime}}(v)
is independent of the chosen
E^{\prime}
, there exists a unique function
F
with
F(v)=F_{E^{\prime}}(v)
for all
v\in C_{E^{\prime}}
. Since
F
is continuous restricted to each
C_{E^{\prime}}
, and the
C_{E^{\prime}}
are finitely many closed sets that cover the space,
F
is continuous.

We saw above that
F_{E^{\prime}}(v)\geq 0
for all
v
and
F_{E^{\prime}}(0)=0
. From Proposition
5.4
it follows that
F(v)\geq 0
for all
v
and
F(0)=0
. On the other hand, since we assumed that the constant coefficient of
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k};E)
is
1
, which agrees with the constant coefficient of
Z(\vec{x},\vec{k}q,\chi,M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}})
, we have
F(v)>0
for any
v
in the interior of the cone
C_{E}
. S This implies that
F
is not a concave function, by considering
0=\frac{1}{2}v+\frac{1}{2}(-v)
.

The final step will be similar to the argument that
F
is continuous, but more subtle. Since
F
is not concave, there must be two points where the inequality for concave functions fails. We can perturb those points so the line segment between them does not intersect the codimension
\geq 2
faces of the
C_{E^{\prime}}
and the inequality still fails. Since we have already checked concavity in the interior of each cone, the line segment cannot lie in the interior of a cone and thus must intersect a boundary between cones. If the line segment intersects multiple boundaries, we can pass to a shorter line segment intersecting a single boundary where the inequality fails. Thus there are points
v
in the interior of a cone
C_{E^{\prime}}
and
w
in the interior of an adjacent cone
C_{s_{i}(E^{\prime})}
such that
F(\lambda v+(1-\lambda)w)<\lambda F(v)+(1-\lambda)F(w)
for some
\lambda\in[0,1]
. Without loss of generality,
\lambda v+(1-\lambda)w\in C_{E^{\prime}}
. We will derive a contradiction from this.

Let the minimum in the definition of
F(\lambda v+(1-\lambda)w))
be attained at
\vec{k},\vec{d}
. We clearly have
F(v)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}v\cdot e_{i}
. Certainly also
d_{i}=d^{\mathrm{min}}
in the sense of Lemma
5.9
. Thus by Lemma
5.9
, the coefficients in in
\widetilde{Z}(\vec{x},\vec{k}^{\prime};s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime}))
and
Z(\vec{x},\vec{k}^{\prime};q,\chi,M^{\psi,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime}),\zeta_{1}})
of the monomial
x_{i}^{d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}}\prod_{j\neq i}x_{i}^{d_{i}}
differ for
d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}\leq\prod_{j\neq i}d_{j}m_{ij}-d_{i}
.

Thus we have

F(w)\leq d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}w\cdot s_{i,E}(e_{i})+\sum_{j\neq i}d_{j}w\cdot s_{i,E}(e_{j})=-d^{{}^{\prime}\mathrm{min}}w\cdot e_{i}+\sum_{j\neq i}d_{j}w\cdot(e_{j}+m_{ij}e_{i})

=(\sum_{j\neq i}d_{j}m_{ij}-d^{{}^{\prime}\textrm{min}})w\cdot e_{i}+\sum_{j\neq i}d_{j}w\cdot e_{j}\leq d_{i}w\cdot e_{i}+\sum_{j\neq i}d_{j}w\cdot e_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}w\cdot e_{j}

since
\sum_{j\neq i}d_{j}m_{ij}-d^{{}^{\prime}\textrm{min}}\geq d_{i}
by Lemma
5.9
and
w\cdot e_{i}\leq 0
.

It follows that

\lambda F(v)+(1-\lambda)F(w)\leq\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}v\cdot e_{j}+(1-\lambda)\sum_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}w\cdot e_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}d_{j}(\lambda v+(1-\lambda)w)\cdot e_{j}=F(\lambda v+(1-\lambda)w)

giving the desired contradiction and completing the proof. ∎

5.2
Examples, prior work, and moments

In this subsection, we list some examples of axiomatic multiple Dirichlet series for which our functional equations imply meromorphicity, focusing on those that appeared already in prior work (or for which a number field analogue appeared), or those potentially useful for moment computations.

The moments of
L
-functions one hopes to study with multiple Dirichlet series are of the form

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{1},\dots,f_{t}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[T]^{+}\\
\deg f_{i}=d_{i}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=t+1}^{r}L(s_{i},\chi^{i}_{f_{1},\dots,f_{t}})

where
\chi^{i}_{f_{1},\dots,f_{t}}
is a Dirichlet character depending on the variables
f_{1},\dots,f_{t}
, defined using some combination of power residue symbols. One studies these using the series

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{1},\dots,f_{t}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[T]^{+}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=t+1}^{r}L(s_{i},\chi^{i}_{f_{1},\dots,f_{t}})\prod_{i=1}^{t}q^{-s_{i}\deg f_{i}}

=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{1},\dots,f_{t}\end{subarray}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[t]^{+}}\Bigl(\sum_{f_{t+1},\dots,f_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[T]^{+}}\prod_{i=t+1}^{r}\chi^{i}_{f_{1},\dots,f_{t}}(f_{i})q^{-s_{i}\deg f_{i}}\Bigr)\prod_{i=1}^{t}q^{-s_{i}\deg f_{i}}

=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f_{1},\dots,f_{r}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[t]^{+}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=t+1}^{r}\chi^{i}_{f_{1},\dots,f_{t}}(f_{i})\prod_{i=1}^{r}q^{-s_{i}\deg f_{i}}

which can be analyzed by comparison with a multiple Dirichlet series whose coefficients agree with
\prod_{i=t+1}^{r}\chi^{i}_{f_{1},\dots,f_{t}}(f_{i})
for
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}
relatively prime. These arise from axiomatic multiple Dirichlet series associated to a matrix
M
such that
M_{ij}=0
if
i,j\leq t
or
i,j>t
. Such series have coefficients of the form
\prod_{i=1}^{t}\prod_{j=t+1}^{r}\left(\frac{f_{j}}{f_{i}}\right)_{\chi}^{M_{ij}}
so that we can take
\chi^{i}_{f_{1},\dots,f_{t}}(f)=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(\frac{f}{f_{i}}\right)_{\chi}^{M_{ij}}
.

Thus, to find axiomatic multiple Dirichlet series of relevance to moments, we can examine the classification of arithmetic root systems for Dynkin diagrams that form a bipartite graph with all vertices labeled with
-1
, as this ensures that the matrix
M
satisfies the vanishing condition.

Example 5.10
.

We consider the multiple Dirichlet series arising from the arithmetic root system
\mathtt{g}(2,3)
. According to
[
1
, §8.3.3]
, this root system has bases with four different generalized Dynkin diagrams.
