\operatorname{Sym}^{\hat{d}}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
6.4
because pushforward of local systems along covering spaces corresponds to induced representations of their fundamental groups. ∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Suppose now that
M=M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}}
for some basis
E
of
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
and bicharacter
\psi
. Then the diagonal braided vector space
V^{\prime}_{\psi,E}
defines a representation
V^{{}^{\prime}\otimes(\hat{d})}_{\psi,E}
of the braid group. Let
\operatorname{Gr}^{\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}}V^{{}^{\prime}\otimes(\hat{d})}_{\psi,E}
be its associated graded component with grade
\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}
.

We have an isomorphism of braid group representations

\operatorname{Gr}^{\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}}V^{{}^{\prime}\otimes(\hat{d})}_{\psi,E}\cong\operatorname{Ind}_{B_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}}}^{B_{\hat{d}}}W.

Proof.

The vector space
V^{{}^{\prime}\otimes(\hat{d})}_{\psi,E}
admits a basis consisting of tensor products of
\hat{d}
-tuples of the
x_{i}
with
x_{i}
graded as
e_{i}
. Thus the component
\operatorname{Gr}^{\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}}V^{{}^{\prime}\otimes(\hat{d})}_{\psi,E}
admits a basis consisting of tensor products where
x_{i}
appears exactly
d_{i}
times (recalling that the
e_{i}
are a basis and hence linearly independent). Each basis vector generates a one-dimensional subspace, whose direct sum is the full vector space, and the action of the braid group sends each one-dimensional subspace to another one-dimensional subspace, with the action on the subspaces given by the homomorphism
B_{n}\to S_{n}
composed with the action of
S_{n}
on
n
-tuples of symbols
x_{1},\dots,x_{r}
where the
i
th symbol occurs
d_{i}
times. This condition, that a representation is a sum of subspaces permuted by the elements of a group, is exactly the condition for the representation to be an induced representation: Specifically, the representation is
\operatorname{Ind}_{H}^{G}W
where
W
is one subspace,
H
is the stabilizer of that subspace, and
G
is the full group.

The stabilizer of the subspace generated by
x_{1}^{\otimes d_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes x_{r}^{\otimes d_{r}}
is the colored braid group on
\hat{d}
strands, with
d_{i}
strands of the
i
th color. A braid where a strand of the
i
th color passes another strand of the
i
th color acts on this subspace as
-\psi(e_{i},e_{i})=\zeta_{1}^{M_{ii}}
. A braid where a strand of the
i
th color loops around a strand of the
j
th color can be expressed as a series of simple braids to bring those two strands next to each other, a simple braid where the strand of the
i
th color crosses over the strand of the
j
th color, a simple braid where the strand of the
j
th color now crosses over the strand of the
i
th color, followed by the inverse of the original sequence of simple braids. Each simple braid induces a scalar multiplication, with the initial sequence and final sequence cancelling, and the two middle steps contributing
-\psi(e_{i},e_{j})
and
-\psi(e_{j},e_{i})
respectively, so this braid acts by multiplication by

(-\psi(e_{i},e-j))(-\psi(e_{j},e_{i}))=\psi(e_{i},e_{j})\psi(e_{j},e_{i})=\zeta_{1}^{M_{ij}}.

This identifies the one-dimensional character with the character
W
. ∎

It follows from Lemma
6.6
that

\displaystyle H^{j-\hat{d}}(\operatorname{Sym}^{\hat{d}}(\mathbb{C}),\overline{u}_{!*}\mathcal{L}\{\operatorname{ind}_{B_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}}}^{B_{\hat{d}}}W\}[\hat{d}])

(6.7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{j-\hat{d}}(\operatorname{Sym}^{\hat{d}}(\mathbb{C}),\overline{u}_{!*}\mathcal{L}\{V^{{}^{\prime}\otimes(\hat{d})}_{\psi,E}[\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}]\}[\hat{d}])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{j-\hat{d}}(\operatorname{Sym}^{\hat{d}}(\mathbb{C}),\overline{u}_{!*}\mathcal{L}\{V^{{}^{\prime}\otimes(\hat{d})}_{\psi,E}\}[\hat{d}])[\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}]

since taking the sheaf associated to a local system, intermediate extension, and cohomology all commute with taking a graded component.

The cohomology of the intermediate extension was essentially computed by Kapranov and Schechtman and this leads to the following lemma:

Lemma 6.7
.

We have (where
\mathbf{1}
is the trivial one-dimensional module)

\operatorname{Gr}^{\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}}H^{j-\hat{d}}(\operatorname{Sym}^{\hat{d}}(\mathbb{C}),\overline{u}_{!*}\mathcal{L}\{V^{{}^{\prime}\otimes(\hat{d})}_{\psi,E}\}[\hat{d}])\cong\Biggl(\operatorname{Gr}^{-\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{\hat{d}-j}_{\mathfrak{B}(V_{\psi,E})}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1})\Biggr)^{\vee}.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to
[
39
, Corollary 3.3.4]
.

Kaparanov and Schechtman define
[
39
, Theorem 3.3.1]
a series of functors
L_{n}
from primitive bialgebras to perverse sheaves on
\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
which combine into a functor
L
to certain tuples of perverse sheaves. It follows from
[
39
, Theorem 3.3.3(c)]
that
L_{\hat{d}}(\mathfrak{B}(V_{\psi,E}))=\overline{u}_{!*}\mathcal{L}\{V^{{}^{\prime}\otimes(\hat{d})}_{\psi,E}\}[\hat{d}]
.

The scaling action of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
on
\mathbb{C}
induces a scaling action of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
on
\mathbb{C}
. The perverse sheaf
\overline{u}_{!*}\mathcal{L}\{V^{{}^{\prime}\otimes(\hat{d})}_{\psi,E}\}[\hat{d}]
is equivariant under this action because it is locally constant on the diagonal stratification which is
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-invariant, so the cohomology
H^{j-\hat{d}}(\operatorname{Sym}^{\hat{d}}(\mathbb{C}),\overline{u}_{!*}\mathcal{L}\{V^{{}^{\prime}\otimes(\hat{d})}_{\psi,E}\}[\hat{d}])
of this complex is isomorphic to the stalk at
0
by localization.

By
[
39
, Theorem 3.3.1(d)]
, the stalk of
\overline{u}_{!*}\mathcal{L}\{V^{{}^{\prime}\otimes(\hat{d})}_{\psi,E}\}[\hat{d}]
at
0
is isomorphic to the cohomology of the bar complex computing
\operatorname{Gr}^{\hat{d}}\operatorname{Tor}_{*}^{\mathfrak{B}(V_{\psi,E})}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1})
, normalized so that
\operatorname{Tor}_{j}
occurs in degree
-j
.

Matching degrees,

H^{j-\hat{d}}(\operatorname{Sym}^{\hat{d}}(\mathbb{C}),\overline{u}_{!*}\mathcal{L}\{V^{{}^{\prime}\otimes(\hat{d})}_{\psi,E}\})=\operatorname{Gr}^{\hat{d}}\operatorname{Tor}_{\hat{d}-j}^{\mathfrak{B}(V_{\psi,E})}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1}).

The relevant
\mathbb{Z}
-grading is related to the
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
-grading by sending a degree
\sum_{i=1}^{r}f_{i}e_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}
to a degree
\sum_{i=1}^{r}f_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. In particular, it sends the degree
\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}
to
\hat{d}
. Thus, taking this Tor group,

\operatorname{Gr}^{\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}}\operatorname{Gr}^{\hat{d}}\operatorname{Tor}_{\hat{d}-j}^{\mathfrak{B}(V_{\psi,E})}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1})=\operatorname{Gr}^{\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}}\operatorname{Tor}_{\hat{d}-j}^{\mathfrak{B}(V_{\psi,E})}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1}).

Hence

\operatorname{Gr}^{\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}}H^{j-\hat{d}}(\operatorname{Sym}^{\hat{d}}(\mathbb{C}),\overline{u}_{!*}\mathcal{L}\{V^{{}^{\prime}\otimes(\hat{d})}_{\psi,E}\}[\hat{d}])\cong\operatorname{Gr}^{\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}}\operatorname{Tor}_{\hat{d}-j}^{\mathfrak{B}(V_{\psi,E})}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1})

\cong\Biggl(\operatorname{Gr}^{-\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{\hat{d}-j}_{\mathfrak{B}(V_{\psi,E})}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1})\Biggr)^{\vee}

by the duality between
\operatorname{Ext}
and
\operatorname{Tor}
(arising from the fact that
\otimes\mathbf{1}
and
\operatorname{Hom}(-,\mathbf{1})
are equivalent as functors from modules to vector spaces after composing with the dual vector space functor). ∎

Corollary 6.8
.

There exists an isomorphism

H^{j}(\mathbb{A}^{d_{1}}\times\dots\times\mathbb{A}^{d_{r}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}})\cong\Biggl(\operatorname{Gr}^{-\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{\hat{d}-j}_{\mathfrak{B}(V_{\psi,E})}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1})\Biggr)^{\vee}

Proof.

This follows by combining (
6.5
), Lemma
6.3
, (
6.6
), Lemma
6.5
, (
6.7
), and Lemma
6.7
.
∎

6.4
Relations between cohomology groups from the functional equations

Theorem 6.9
.

Fix a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and a character
\chi\colon\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
of order
n
. Let
r
be a natural number and
M
an
r\times r
symmetric matrix of integers in
[0,n)
. Let
K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}}^{M}
be the shifted perverse sheaf on
\mathbb{A}^{d_{1}}\times\dots\times\mathbb{A}^{d_{r}}=\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}
defined using the matrix
M
.

Fix
i
from
1
to
r
and nonnegative integers
d_{0},\dots,d_{i-1},d_{i+1},\dots,d_{r}
.
Then

1.

If
\gcd(n,M_{ii}+M_{ij}/2)\mid M_{ij}
for all
j
, let
n_{i}=\frac{n}{\gcd(n,M_{ii}+\frac{n}{2})}
and for all
j
let
n_{ij}
be the unique integer in
[0,n_{i})
with
-M_{ij}\equiv n_{ij}(M_{ii}+\frac{n}{2})\bmod n
. Let

h^{j}_{d_{i}}=\dim H^{\hat{d}-j}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},K^{M}_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}})

and
\widetilde{d}=\sum_{j\neq i}d_{j}n_{ij}
.
Then we have

h^{j}_{d_{i}}-h^{j-1}_{d_{i}-(2d_{i}-1-\widetilde{d})\%n_{i}}=h^{j}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{i}-(\widetilde{d}+1-2d_{i})\%n_{i}}-h^{j-1}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{i}-n_{i}}

(6.8)

unless
2d_{i}\equiv 1+\widetilde{d}\bmod n_{i}
, in which case

h^{j}_{d_{i}}=h^{j}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{i}-n_{i}}.

(6.9)

2.

If
\psi(e_{i},e_{i})=-1
, let

\widetilde{d}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\neq i\\
M_{ij}\neq 0\end{subarray}}d_{j}.

let

h^{j,M}_{d_{i}}=\dim H^{\hat{d}-j}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},K^{M}_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}})

and

h^{j,\tau_{i}(M)}_{d_{i}}=\dim H^{\hat{d}-j}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},K^{\tau_{i}(M)}_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}})

with
\tau_{i}(M)
defined as in §
4.2
.
Then

h^{j,M}_{d_{i}}=h^{j,\tau_{i}(M)}_{\widetilde{d}-d_{i}-1}

(6.10)

unless
\sum_{k\neq i}d_{k}M_{ik}\equiv 0\bmod n
, in which case

h^{j,M}_{d_{i}}-h^{j-1,M}_{d_{i}-1}=h^{j,\tau_{i}(M)}_{\widetilde{d}-d_{i}}-h^{j-1,\tau_{i}(M)}_{\widetilde{d}-d_{i}-1}.

(6.11)

Proof.

Applying a permutation of
\{1,\dots,r\}
preserves everything except that the polynomial
F_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}}
may be multiplied by
-1
which has the effect of taking the tensor product of
K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}}
with a one-dimensional Galois representation. This does not affect the Betti numbers. Hence we may without loss of generality assume
i=r
. We now handle each case in turn.

In case (1), consider the local series, specializing to
\pi=T
,

D_{T}(x,T^{\vec{d}},k)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d_{r}\geq 0\\
d_{r}\equiv k\bmod n\end{subarray}}a(T^{d_{1}},\dots,T^{d_{r}})x^{d_{r}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d_{r}\geq 0\\
d_{r}\equiv k\bmod n\end{subarray}}\sum_{j}(-1)^{j}\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{Frob}_{q},H^{j}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}}},K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}})x^{d_{r}}.

(6.12)

Then the local functional equation (
3.10
), established in Theorem
3.9
, states that
D_{T}^{*}(x,T^{\vec{d}},k)
is a rational function of
x
with denominator dividing
1-q^{-1}\left(\frac{qx}{g(1,T)}\right)^{n_{r}}
and

\left(D_{T}(x,T^{\vec{d}},k)\right)_{k}=\left(\frac{qx}{g}\right)^{\widetilde{d}}\left(\Gamma_{T,k,\ell}(x,\widetilde{d})\right)_{k,\ell}\left(D_{T}^{*}(\frac{g^{2}}{q^{2}x},T^{\vec{d}},\ell)\right)_{\ell}

(6.13)

where
\left(\Gamma_{T,k,\ell}(x,\widetilde{d})\right)_{k,\ell}
is the
n_{r}\times n_{r}
scattering matrix with

\begin{split}\Gamma_{T,k,k}=&\left(\frac{qx}{g}\right)^{1-(\widetilde{d}+1-2k)\%n_{r}}\frac{1-q^{-1}}{1-q^{-1}\left(\frac{qx}{g}\right)^{n_{r}}}\end{split}

\begin{split}\Gamma_{T,k,\ell}=&(\xi\chi^{M_{rr}})^{\ell(1+\widetilde{d})}(-1)\frac{g_{(\xi\chi^{M_{rr}})^{2k-\widetilde{d}-1}}}{q}\\
&\left(\frac{qx}{g}\right)\frac{1-\left(\frac{qx}{g}\right)^{-n_{r}}}{1-q^{-1}\left(\frac{qx}{g}\right)^{n_{r}}}\end{split}

for
\ell\equiv 1+\widetilde{d}-k\bmod n
, and all other entries equal to
0
. In addition, there are special entries in the matrix when
k\equiv\ell\equiv 1+\widetilde{d}-k\bmod n
. In this case

\Gamma_{k,\ell}=\left(\frac{qx}{g}\right)^{1-n_{r}}

We may clear denominators by multiplying both sides by
1-q^{-1}\left(\frac{qx}{g}\right)^{n_{r}}
, which makes the left side, and hence the right side, a polynomial in
x
. After doing this, we have an equality of coefficients of each power of
x
.

Each coefficient of a power of
x
in the expression on both sides is a signed sum of Weil numbers. Furthermore, the analogous coefficients for the Dirichlet series over
\mathbb{F}_{q^{e}}
are obtained by taking the signed sums of the
e
th powers of the Weil numbers. Since the same identity holds over
\mathbb{F}_{q^{e}}
for each
e
, it follows that the same Weil numbers, with the same multiplicities, must appear in the coefficient of each power of
x
on both sides. We now introduce a formal variable
u
and consider the weight-extraction operation that replaces a Weil number of absolute value
q^{w/2}
by
(-u)^{w}
. Weight-extraction is a ring homomorphism from the ring of sets of ordered pairs of Weil numbers and integers to the ring of polynomials in
u
with integer coefficients.

The cohomology group
H^{j}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}})
is pure, i.e. all Frobenius eigenvalues on it have size
q^{j/2}
. This is because
K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}}
is pure so the eigenvalues of Frobenius on its stalk in degree
j
have size
\leq q^{j/2}
, while the eigenvalues of Frobenius on its global cohomology have size
\geq q^{j/2}
, but these are equal by
[
45
, Lemma 2.16]
and thus all have size exactly
q^{j/2}
.
1
1

1

This observation was pointed out to one of the authors by Kevin Chang.

Hence applying weight-extraction to the set of eigenvalues of Frobenius acting on
H^{j}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}})
, i.e. the set of Weil numbers contributing to
\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{Frob}_{q},H^{j}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}}))
, produces
(-u)^{j}\dim H^{j}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}}))
. Thus applying weight-extraction to the set of ordered pairs of Weil numbers and integers computing
\sum_{j}(-1)^{j}\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{Frob}_{q},H^{j}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}}))
produces
\sum_{j}u^{j}\dim H^{j}_{c}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}}))
, i.e., the Poincaré polynomial.

Also, weight-extraction sends
q
to
u^{2}
,
g
to
u
, and
\chi(-1)
to
1
. Hence the Dirichlet series

W(x,u,\vec{d},k)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}\\
d_{r}\equiv k\bmod n\end{subarray}}\sum_{j}u^{j}\dim H^{j}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}}))x^{d_{r}}

satisfies the functional equation

\left(\left(1-u^{-2}\left(ux\right)^{n_{r}}\right)W(x,u,\vec{d},k)\right)_{k}=(ux)^{\widetilde{d}}\left(\Omega_{k,\ell}(x,u,\widetilde{d})\right)_{k,\ell}\left(W\left(\frac{1}{u^{2}x},u,\vec{d},\ell\right)\right)_{\ell}

(6.14)

where
\left(\Omega_{k,\ell}(x,u,\widetilde{d})\right)_{k,\ell}
is the
n_{r}\times n_{r}
scattering matrix with

\Omega_{k,k}=\left(ux\right)^{1-(\widetilde{d}+1-2k)\%n_{r}}(1-u^{-2})

\begin{split}\Omega_{k,\ell}=&x\left(1-\left(ux\right)^{-n_{r}}\right)\end{split}

for
\ell\equiv 1+\widetilde{d}-k\bmod n
, and all other entries equal to
0
. In addition, there are special entries in the matrix when
k\equiv\ell\equiv 1+\widetilde{d}-k\bmod n
. In this case

\Omega_{k,\ell}=\left(1-u^{2}\left(ux\right)^{n_{r}}\right)\left(ux\right)^{1-n_{r}}

We can simplify the functional equation further. The renormalized series

\overline{W}(x,u,\vec{d},k)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}\\
d_{r}\equiv k\bmod n\end{subarray}}\sum_{j}u^{j}\dim H^{j}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}}))(u^{-1}x)^{d_{r}},

where we have substituted
u^{-1}x
for
x
, satisfies the functional equation

\left(\left(1-u^{-2}x^{n_{r}}\right)\overline{W}(x,u,\vec{d},k)\right)_{k}=\left(\overline{\Omega}_{k,\ell}(x,u,\widetilde{d})\right)_{k,\ell}\left(\overline{W}\left(x^{-1},u,\vec{d},\ell\right)\right)_{\ell}

(6.15)

where
\left(\overline{\Omega}_{k,\ell}(x,u,\widetilde{d})\right)_{k,\ell}
is the
n\times n
scattering matrix with

\overline{\Omega}_{k,k}=x^{\widetilde{d}+1-(\widetilde{d}+1-2k)\%n_{r}}\left(1-u^{-2}\right)

\begin{split}\overline{\Omega}_{k,\ell}=&u^{-1}x^{\widetilde{d}+1}\left(1-x^{-n_{r}}\right)\end{split}

for
\ell\equiv 1+\widetilde{d}-k\bmod n
, and all other entries equal to
0
. In addition, there are special entries in the matrix when
k\equiv\ell\equiv 1+\widetilde{d}-k\bmod n
. In this case

\overline{\Omega}_{k,\ell}=\left(1-u^{-2}x^{n_{r}}\right)x^{\widetilde{d}+1-n_{r}}

Extracting the coefficient of
u^{d_{1}+\dots+d_{r-1}-j}x^{d_{r}}
on each side and recalling that
h^{j}_{d_{r}}=\dim H^{\hat{d}-j}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}})
, we see that

h^{j}_{d_{r}}-h^{j-2}_{d_{r}-{n_{r}}}=h^{j-1}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}}-h^{j-1}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}-n_{r}}+h^{j}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}-(\widetilde{d}+1-2d_{r})\%n_{r}}-h^{j-2}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}-(\widetilde{d}+1-2d_{r})\%n_{r}}

(6.16)

unless
2d_{r}\equiv 1+\widetilde{d}\bmod n_{r}
, in which case

h^{j}_{d_{r}}-h^{j-2}_{d_{r}-n_{r}}=h^{j}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}-n_{r}}-h^{j-2}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}}.

(6.17)

Both of these equations may be simplified by a telescoping sum.
If we substitute
j-2k
for
j
and
d_{r}-n_{r}k
for
d_{r}
into (
6.17
) we obtain

h^{j-2k}_{d_{r}-kn_{r}}-h^{j-2(k+1)}_{d_{r}-(k+1)n_{r}}=h^{j-2k}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}+(k-1)n_{r}}-h^{j-2(k+1)}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}+kn_{r}}

(6.18)

and then summing (
6.18
) over
k
from
0
to
\infty
we obtain

\displaystyle h^{j}_{d_{r}}=

\displaystyle(h^{j}_{d_{r}}-h^{j-2}_{d_{r}-n_{r}})+(h^{j-2}_{d_{r}-n_{r}}-h^{j-4}_{d_{r}-2n_{r}})+\dots

(6.19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(h^{j}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}-n_{r}}-h^{j-2}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}})+(h^{j-2}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}}-h^{j-4}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}+n_{r}})+\dots=h^{j}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}-n_{r}}.

Note that all
h^{j}_{d}
with
j<0
vanish so the sum has only finitely many nonvanishing terms and thus our rearrangement is justified. (
6.19
) gives (
6.9
).

If we set
d_{r}^{\prime}=d_{r}-(2d_{r}-1-\widetilde{d})\%n_{r}
then we see that
d_{r}+(\widetilde{d}+1-2d_{r})=d_{r}^{\prime}+n_{r}
and that

d_{r}^{\prime\prime}=d_{r}-(2d_{r}-1-\widetilde{d})\%n_{r}-(2d_{r}^{\prime}-1-\widetilde{d})\%n_{r}=d_{r}-(2d_{r}-1-\widetilde{d})\%n_{r}-(1+\widetilde{d}-2d_{r})\%n_{r}=d_{r}-n_{r}

so we can rewrite (
6.16
) as

h^{j}_{d_{r}}-h^{j-2}_{d_{r}-{n_{r}}}=h^{j-1}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}}-h^{j-1}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}-n_{r}}+h^{j}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}^{\prime}-n_{r}}-h^{j-2}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}^{\prime}-n_{r}}.

(6.20)

Substituting
j-2k
for
j
and
d_{r}-n_{r}k
for
d_{r}
into (
6.20
) we obtain

h^{j-2k}_{d_{r}-kn_{r}}-h^{j-2k-2}_{d_{r}-(k+1)n_{r}}=h^{j-2k-1}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}+kn_{r}}-h^{j-2k-1}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}+(k-1)n_{r}}+h^{j-2k}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}^{\prime}+(k-1)n_{r}}-h^{j-2k-2}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}^{\prime}+(k-1)n_{r}}.

(6.21)

Substituting
j-2k-1
for
j
and
d_{r}^{\prime}-n_{r}k
for
d_{r}
into (
6.20
) we obtain

h^{j-2k-1}_{d_{r}^{\prime}-kn_{r}}-h^{j-2k-3}_{d_{r}^{\prime}-(k+1)n_{r}}=h^{j-2k-2}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}^{\prime}+kn_{r}}-h^{j-2k-2}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}^{\prime}+(k-1)n_{r}}+h^{j-2k-1}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}+kn_{r}}-h^{j-2k-3}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}+kn_{r}}.

(6.22)

Summing (
6.21
) over
k
from
0
to
\infty
together with minus (
6.22
) over
k
from
0
to
\infty
we obtain

\displaystyle h^{j}_{d_{r}}-h^{j-1}_{d_{r}^{\prime}}

(6.23)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(h^{j}_{d_{r}}-h^{j-2}_{d_{r}-n_{r}})-(h^{j-1}_{d_{r}^{\prime}}-h^{j-3}_{d_{r}^{\prime}-n_{r}})+(h^{j-2}_{d_{r}-n_{r}}-h^{j-4}_{d_{r}-4n_{r}})-(h^{j-3}_{d_{r}^{\prime}+n_{r}}-h^{j-5}_{d_{r}^{\prime}+2n_{r}})+\dots

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(h^{j-1}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}}-h^{j-1}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}-n_{r}}+h^{j}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}^{\prime}-n_{r}}-h^{j-2}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}^{\prime}-n_{r}})-(h^{j-2}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}^{\prime}}-h^{j-2}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}^{\prime}-n_{r}}+h^{j-1}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}}-h^{j-3}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(h^{j-3}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}+n_{r}}-h^{j-3}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}}+h^{j-2}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}^{\prime}}-h^{j-4}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}^{\prime}})-(h^{j-4}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}^{\prime}+n_{r}}-h^{j-4}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}^{\prime}}+h^{j-3}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}+n_{r}}-h^{j-5}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}+n_{r}})+\dots

\displaystyle=-h^{j-1}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}-n_{r}}+h^{j}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}^{\prime}-n_{r}}.

Again only finitely many summands are nonzero so rearrangement is justified, and now (
6.23
) implies (
6.8
).

We now consider the second case. Note that the definition of
M^{\prime}
matches
\tau_{r}(M)
.

Consider the local series

D_{T}(x,T^{\vec{d}};M)=\sum_{d_{r}=0}^{\infty}a(T^{d_{1}},\dots,T^{d_{r}})x^{d_{r}};q,\chi,M)=\sum_{d_{r}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j}(-1)^{j}\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{Frob}_{q},H^{j}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}}},K^{M}_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}})x^{d_{r}}.

(6.24)

and similarly

D_{T}(x,T^{\vec{d}};M^{\prime})=\sum_{d_{r}=0}^{\infty}a(T^{d_{1}},\dots,T^{d_{r}})x^{d_{r}};q,\chi,M^{\prime})=\sum_{d_{r}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j}(-1)^{j}\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{Frob}_{q},H^{j}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}_{q}}},K^{M^{\prime}}_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}})x^{d_{r}}.

(6.25)

Then the local functional equation (
4.10
), established by Theorem
4.9
, states that

\begin{split}D_{T}(x,T^{\vec{d}};M)&=\omega_{T}\left(\frac{g_{\chi^{\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}d_{i}M_{ir}}}}{q}\right)^{b_{T}-1}(\chi(-1)^{\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}d_{i}M_{ir}}qx)^{\widetilde{d}-1}\\
&\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r-1\\
M_{ir}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{g_{\chi^{M_{ir}}}}{q}\right)^{d_{i}}\left(\frac{qx-1}{1-x}\right)^{b_{T}}D_{T}(\frac{1}{qx},T^{\vec{d}};M^{\prime})\end{split}

(6.26)

where
b_{T}=1
if
n\mid\sum_{i=1}^{r-1}d_{i}M_{ir}
and
0
otherwise, and

\omega_{T}=\omega_{T}(T^{\vec{d}};M)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r-1\\
M_{ir}\neq 0\end{subarray}}(-1)^{d_{i}(d_{i}-1)(q-1)/4}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq r-1\\
M_{ir},M_{jr}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\chi(-1)^{d_{i}d_{j}M_{ir}}=\pm 1.

(6.27)

The functional equation also states that
D_{T}(x,T^{\vec{d}};M)
and
D_{T}(x,T^{\vec{d}};M^{\prime})
are rational functions with denominator dividing
(1-x)^{b_{T}}
. Hence we may multiply both sides by
(1-x)^{b_{T}}
to obtain an equation bewteen polynomials in
x
. After doing this, we have an equality of coefficients of each power of
x
.

We now define the Dirichlet series

W(x,u,\vec{d};M)=\sum_{d_{r}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j}u^{j}\dim H^{j}(\mathbb{A}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}^{\hat{d}},K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}}^{M}x^{d_{r}})

Since the above functional equation holds over
\mathbb{F}_{q^{e}}
for each
e
, we may apply the weight-extraction operation, exactly as in the previous argument, to obtain the functional equation

(1-x)^{b_{T}}W(x,u,\vec{d};M)=\left(\frac{u}{u^{2}}\right)^{b_{T}-1}(u^{2}x)^{\widetilde{d}-1}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq r-1\\
M_{ir}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{u}{u^{2}}\right)^{d_{i}}\left(u^{2}x-1\right)^{b_{T}}W(\frac{1}{u^{2}x},u,\vec{d};M^{\prime})

which further simplifies to

(1-x)^{b_{T}}W(x,u,\vec{d};M)=u^{-b_{T}}(ux)^{\widetilde{d}-1}d_{i}\left(u^{2}x-1\right)^{b_{T}}W(\frac{1}{u^{2}x},u,\vec{d};M^{\prime}).

For the renormalized Dirichlet series

\overline{W}(x,u,\vec{d};M)=\sum_{d_{r}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j}u^{j}\dim H^{j}(\mathbb{A}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}^{\hat{d}},K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}}^{M}(u^{-1}x)^{d_{r}})

we obtain

(1-u^{-1}x)^{b_{T}}\overline{W}(x,u,\vec{d};M)=u^{-b_{T}}x^{\widetilde{d}-1}\left(ux-1\right)^{b_{T}}\overline{W}(\frac{1}{x},u,\vec{d};M^{\prime}).

Extracting the coefficient of
u^{d_{1}+\dots+d_{r-1}-j}x^{d_{r}}
on each side and recalling that
h^{j,M}_{d_{r}}=\dim H^{\hat{d}-j}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}}^{M})
and
h^{j,M^{\prime}}_{d_{r}}=\dim H^{\hat{d}-j}(\mathbb{A}^{\hat{d}}_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}},K_{d_{1},\dots,d_{r}}^{M^{\prime}})
, we obtain

h^{j,M}_{d_{r}}=h^{j,M^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{d}-1-d_{r}}

if
b_{T}=0
and

h^{j,M}_{d_{r}}-h^{j-1,M}_{d_{r}-1}=h^{j,M^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{d}-d_{r}}-h^{j-1,M^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{d}-1-d_{r}}

otherwise. These give (
6.10
) and (
6.11
) respectively.
∎

Proof of Corollary
6.1
.

This follows immediately from Theorem
6.9
, taking
M=M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}}
, by the following reasoning: In case 1, Corollary
6.8
shows that
h^{j}_{d_{i}}
as defined here and in Theorem
6.9
agree, and Lemma
5.16

m_{ik}=n_{ik}
so that the definitions of
\widetilde{d}
agree. In case 2, Lemma
5.17
shows that
\tau_{i}(M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}})=(M^{\psi,E,\zeta_{1}})^{\prime}
so that Corollary
6.8
gives
h^{j,M}_{d_{i}}=h^{j,E}_{d_{i}}
and
h^{j,\tau_{i}(M)}_{d_{i}}=h^{j,s_{i}(E)}_{d_{i}}
, and
\widetilde{d}
agrees by Lemma
5.17
.∎

The results of §
6.3
allow us to give several other equivalent definitions of
h^{j}_{d_{j}}
and thus several other variants of Theorem
6.9
, but we do not state all of these.

We also raise the question of whether a generalized relation holds.

Question 6.10
.

Let
r
be a natural number,
\psi\colon\mathbb{Z}^{r}\times\mathbb{Z}^{r}\to\mu_{\infty}
a bicharacter, and
E=(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})
an ordered basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
.

Fix
i
from
1
to
r
assume
E
is admissible at
i
. Fix nonnegative integers
d_{0},\dots,d_{i-1},d_{i+1},\dots,d_{r}
. Let
\widetilde{d}=\sum_{k\neq i}d_{k}m_{ik}
. Let
B\mathfrak{B}(V_{\psi,E})
be the Nichols algebra of the diagonal braided vector space defined using
\psi
and
E
as in §
6.2
. Let

h^{j,E}_{d_{i}}=\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{-\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{j}_{\mathfrak{B}(V_{\psi,E})}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1})

and

h^{j,s_{i,E}(E)}_{d_{i}}=\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{-\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{j}_{\mathfrak{B}(V_{\psi,s_{i,E}(E)})}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1}).

Does the following system of equations always hold? We have

h^{j,E}_{d_{i}}=h^{j,s_{i,E}(E)}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{i}-n_{i}},

(6.28)

unless there exists an integer
e
with
\prod_{k\neq i}(\psi(e_{i},e_{k})\psi(e_{k},e_{i}))^{d_{k}}=\psi(e_{i},e_{i})^{e}
, in which case we have

h^{j,E}_{d_{i}}-h^{j-1,E}_{d_{i}-(2d_{i}+e-1)\%n_{i}}=h^{j,s_{i,E}(E)}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{i}-(1-e-2d_{i})\%n_{i}}-h^{j-1,s_{i,E}(E)}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{r}-n_{r}},

(6.29)

unless
2d_{i}+e\equiv 1\bmod n_{i}
, in which case we again have

h^{j,E}_{d_{i}}=h^{j,s_{i,E}(E)}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{i}-n_{i}}.

(6.30)

Finally, we show that Corollary
6.1
uniquely determines the graded dimensions of cohomology groups of Nichols algebras. It will be clear from Theorem
6.11
that there exists a purely combinatorial algorithm to compute these Betti numbers.

Theorem 6.11
.

Let
(\mathbf{\Delta},\psi,E)
be an arithmetic root system such that each root
e
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
satisfies either condition (1) or (2) in Corollary
6.1
.

The there exists a unique tuple of, for each
E^{\prime}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})\in B_{\psi,E}
, a function
h_{E^{\prime}}=h_{E^{\prime}}(j,d_{1},\dots,d_{r})
from
r+1
-tuples of integers to nonnegative integers, which vanishes if an argument is negative, such that, for each
E^{\prime}\in B_{\psi,E}
,
i
from
1
to
r
, and nonnegative integers
d_{1},\dots,d_{i-1},d_{i+1},\dots,d_{r}
, letting

h^{j,E^{\prime}}_{d_{i}}=h_{E^{\prime}}(j,d_{1},\dots,d_{r})\textrm{ and }h^{j,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})}_{d_{i}}=h_{s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})}(j,d_{1},\dots,d_{r}),

we have the following:

1.

If
\psi(e_{i},a)\psi(a,e_{i})
is a power of
\psi(e_{i},e_{i})
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}
, let

h^{j}_{d_{i}}=h_{E^{\prime}}(j,d_{1},\dots,d_{r}).

Then for all
d_{i}
we have

h^{j,E^{\prime}}_{d_{i}}-h^{j-1,E^{\prime}}_{d_{i}-(2d_{i}-1-\widetilde{d})\%n_{i}}=h^{j,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{i}-(\widetilde{d}+1-2d_{i})\%n_{i}}-h^{j-1,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{i}-n_{i}}

(6.31)

unless
2d_{i}\equiv 1+\widetilde{d}\bmod n_{i}
, in which case

h^{j,E^{\prime}}_{d_{i}}=h^{j,E^{\prime}}_{\widetilde{d}+1-d_{i}-n_{i}}.

(6.32)

2.

If
\psi(e_{i},e_{i})=-1
, then for all
d_{i}
we have (
6.3
) or (
6.4
) as appropriate.

and such that
h^{E^{\prime}}(j,0,\dots,0)=\begin{cases}1&\textrm{if }j=0\\
0&\textrm{if }j\neq 0\end{cases}
for all
E^{\prime}
.

That tuple is given by

h_{E^{\prime}}(j,d_{1},\dots,d_{r})=\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{-\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{j}_{\mathfrak{B}(V_{\psi,E^{\prime}})}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1}).

Proof.

That
\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{-\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{j}_{\mathfrak{B}(V_{\psi,E^{\prime}})}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1})
satisfies these relations is the content of (
6.1
), once one observes that in case (1) we have
h^{j,s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime})}=h^{j,E^{\prime}}
as both of these have the same values of
\psi(e_{i},e_{i})
and
\psi(e_{i},e_{j})\psi(e_{j},e_{i})
for all
i,j
by Lemma
5.16
. Furthermore, that

\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{0}\operatorname{Ext}^{j}_{\mathfrak{B}(V_{\psi,E^{\prime}})}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1})=\begin{cases}1&\textrm{if }j=0\\
0&\textrm{if }j\neq 0\end{cases}

is clear from the fact that
\operatorname{Ext}^{0}
is
1
and that the Ext groups are computed by the bar complex, from which we may see that all higher Ext groups have nonzero grades. Thus
\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{-\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}e_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{j}_{\mathfrak{B}(V_{\psi,E^{\prime}})}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1})
satisfies all the conditions, and so it suffices to show that a solution is unique.

We show that each value of
h_{E^{\prime}}(j,d_{1},\dots,d_{r})
is uniquely determined by the conditions by induction on
j
. In other words, we may assume that
h_{E^{\prime}}(j-1,d_{1},\dots,d_{r})
is uniquely determined for all
d_{1},\dots,d_{r}
. (We take
j=-1
to be the base case, where the unique determination holds by assumption.) Examining each of the relations (
6.31
), (
6.32
), (
6.3
), (
6.4
), we see that each one involves a single
h^{j}
on the left hand side, a single
h^{j}
on the right hand side, possibly some
h^{j-1}
s, and no other terms. Since we have assumed that the
h^{j-1}
terms are uniquely determined, we see that the
h^{j}
term on the left hand side is uniquely determined if and only if the
h^{j}
term on the right hand side is, since one term in a linear equation is uniquely determined if all the other terms are.

Thus we may form a graph whose vertices are the set of tuples
\{E^{\prime},d_{1},\dots,d_{r}\}
with
E^{\prime}\in B_{\psi,E}
and
d_{1},\dots,d_{r}
integers, where two vertices
\{E^{\prime},d_{1},\dots,d_{i-1},d_{i},d_{i+1},\dots,d_{r}\}
and
\{s_{i,E^{\prime}}(E^{\prime}),d_{1},\dots,d_{i-1},d_{i}^{\prime},d_{i+1},\dots,d_{r}\}
with
E=(e_{1},\dots,e_{r})
are connected for
d_{i}^{\prime}
given by:

1.

\psi(e_{i},a)\psi(a,e_{i})
is a power of
\psi(e_{i},e_{i})
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}
and, letting
n_{i}
be the order of
\chi(e_{i},e_{i})
, we have
d_{i}^{\prime}=\widetilde{d}+1-d_{i}-(\widetilde{d}+1-2d_{i})\%n_{i}
, unless
2d_{i}\equiv 1+\widetilde{d}\bmod n_{i}
, which case
d_{i}^{\prime}=\widetilde{d}+1-d_{i}-n_{i}
. (Or, more simply, we may put
d_{i}^{\prime}=\widetilde{d}-d_{i}-(\widetilde{d}-2d_{i})\%n_{i}
.)

2.

\psi(e_{i},e_{i})=-1
, then
d_{i}^{\prime}=\widetilde{d}-d_{i}-1
, unless
\prod_{k\neq i}(\psi(e_{i},e_{k})\psi(e_{k},e_{i}))^{d_{k}}=1
, in which case
d_{i}^{\prime}=\widetilde{d}-d_{i}
.

It suffices to prove that each vertex of the graph is connected to some vertex
(E^{\prime},d_{1},\dots,d_{r})
where either some
d_{i}
is negative or all
d_{i}
vanish, as these values of
h_{E^{\prime}}
are uniquely determined. In fact, the only thing we will use about these formulas is that
d_{i}^{\prime}\leq\widetilde{d}-d_{i}
.

To do this, fix a vertex
(E^{\prime},d_{1},\dots,d_{r})
. If one of
d_{1},\dots,d_{r}
is negative or
d_{1},\dots,d_{r}
are all zero, we are done. Otherwise, choose a point
x
in the interior of the corresponding cone
C_{E^{\prime}}
. Choose a point
y
such that the nonzero linear form
\sum_{k=1}^{r}d_{k}v\cdot e_{k}
on
\mathbb{R}^{r}
takes a negative value at
y
, and the line between
x
and
y
does not intersect the codimension
\geq 2
faces of the cones. The line between
x
and
y
passes through some sequence of cones
E_{1}=E^{\prime},\dots,E_{m}
. Since
E_{t}
is adjacent to
E_{t+1}
, it must be connected by some face, say the
i_{t}
’th face. Define
(d_{1}^{t},\dots,d_{r}^{r})
inductively by
(d_{1}^{0},\dots,d_{r}^{0})=(d_{1},\dots,d_{r})
and

(d_{1}^{t+1},\dots,d^{r}_{t+1})=(d_{1}^{t},\dots,d_{i_{t}-1}^{t},(d_{i_{t}}^{t})^{\prime},d_{i_{t}-1}^{t},\dots,d_{r}^{t}).

Consider the piecewise linear function
F
on the line segment from
x
to
y
which is equal to
v\mapsto\sum_{k=1}^{r}d_{k}^{t}v\cdot e_{k}^{t}
in the region where the line segment intersects the cone
E_{t}=(e_{1}^{t},\dots,e_{r}^{t})
. Let us check that
F
is concave. Since
F
agrees with the linear form
\sum_{k=1}^{r}d_{k}v\cdot e_{k}
in the first part of the line segment, this will imply that
F
bounded above by that linear form in the whole line segment, and in particular negative at
y
, so some
d_{k}^{m}
is negative, giving the desired claim since each
(E^{t},d_{1}^{t},\dots,d_{r}^{r})
is connected to the next by construction.

To check
F
is concave, it suffices to check locally at a neighborhood of each point where
F
fails to be linear, i.e. in a neighborhood of the boundary between the cones
C_{E_{t}}
and
C_{E_{t+1}}
for each
t
. On
C_{E_{t}}
,
F(v)
is given by the formula

F(v)=\sum_{k=1}^{r}d_{k}^{t}v\cdot e_{k}^{t}

and we have
e_{k}^{t+1}=s_{i,E_{t}}(e_{k}^{t})
so that on
C_{E_{t+1}}
,
F(v)
is given by the formula

F(v)=\sum_{k=1}^{r}d_{k}^{t+1}v\cdot e_{k}^{t+1}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}d_{k}^{t+1}v\cdot s_{i,E_{t}}(e_{k}^{t})=d_{i}^{t^{\prime}}v\cdot s_{i,E_{t}}(e_{i}^{t})+\sum_{k\neq i}d_{k}^{t}v\cdot s_{i,E_{t}}(e_{k}^{t})=-d_{i}^{t^{\prime}}v\cdot e_{i}^{t}+\sum_{k\neq i}d_{k}^{t}v\cdot(e_{k}^{t}+m_{ik}e_{i})

=(-d_{i}^{t^{\prime}}+\sum_{k\neq i}m_{ik}d_{k}^{t})v\cdot e_{k}^{t}+\sum_{k\neq i}d_{k}^{t}v\cdot e_{k}^{t}.

Since
d_{i}^{t^{\prime}}\leq\widetilde{d}^{t}-d_{i}^{t}=\sum_{k\neq i}m_{ik}d_{k}^{t}-d_{i}^{t}
and
v\cdot e_{k}^{t}\leq 0
for
v\in C_{E_{t+1}}
, we get

F(v)\leq\sum_{k=1}^{r}d_{k}^{t}v\cdot e_{k}^{t}

on
C_{E_{t+1}}
. On the boundary between
C_{E_{t}}
and
C_{E_{t+1}}
, we have
v\cdot e_{k}=0
so both formulas agree.

Hence, in a neighborhood of the boundary between the cones
C_{E_{t}}
and
C_{E_{t+1}}
,
F
has two linear segments which meet at the boundary point, and one linear segement is above the line formed by extending the other. This means
F
is concave in this neighborhood. Since this works for an arbitrary boundary point,
F
is concave everywhere, as desired.∎

6.5
A toy model with Lie algebras

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a semisimple Lie algebra over the complex numbers with rank
r
. Fix a Cartan and a Borel subalgebra. Let
\Lambda
be the root lattice, a rank
r
lattice. For
\alpha\in\Lambda
, let
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
be the subspace of
\mathfrak{g}
with eigenvalue
\alpha
(which is
1
-dimensional if
\alpha
is a root,
r
-dimensional if
\alpha=0
, and
0
-dimensional otherwise). Let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r}
be the simple roots.

Let
\mathfrak{n}
be the nilpotent part of the Borel subalgebra, i.e. the subspace spanned by
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
for all positive roots
\alpha
, equivalently, the sub-Lie-algebra generated by
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha_{1}},\dots,\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha_{r}}
. The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{n}
is naturally
\Lambda
-graded. Kostant gave the following formula for the Lie algebra cohomology
H^{*}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})=\operatorname{Ext}^{*}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1})
in terms of the Weyl group of
\mathfrak{g}
.

Theorem 6.12
.

(Kostant) We have
\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})=0
unless
\beta=w(\rho)-\rho
for
\rho
half the sum of the positive roots and
k=\operatorname{length}(w)
for some
w
in the Weyl group, and
H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})[\beta]
is
1
-dimensional in that case.

It follows from Theorem
6.12
that the
\beta
-graded piece of the cohomology of
\mathfrak{n}
admit the following symmetry under a shifted action of the Weyl group on
\Lambda
:

Proposition 6.13
.

For
\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle>-\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle
there exists an isomorphism

\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})\to\operatorname{Gr}^{w_{i}(\beta)-\alpha_{i}}H^{k+1}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})

and for
\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle<0
there exists an isomorphism

\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}H^{k+1}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})\to\operatorname{Gr}^{w_{i}(\beta)-\alpha_{i}}H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1}).

Furthermore if
-\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle<\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle<0
then both sides of these isomorphisms vanish.

We give a proof of Theorem
6.12
in two steps. First, we prove Proposition
6.13
by producing an explicit isomorphism. We then show there is a unique vector space graded by
\Lambda
and cohomological degree which admits these symmetries, and that it is given by Theorem
6.12
.

In fact Kostant proved a more general formula, which calculates the cohomology of
\mathfrak{n}
with coefficients in an irreducible representation of
\mathfrak{g}
. The analogue of Theorem
6.13
can still be proven in this context by the same argument, but no longer contains enough information to deduce the analogue of Theorem
6.12
.

Thus the main purpose of the proof is to give context for the algebraic meaning of Corollary
6.1
. It is possible that this proof gives a template for a purely algebraic proof of Corollary
6.1
but we will show in the next section that there is an obstruction to a direct translation of this proof.

The method is as follows. Fix a simple root
\alpha_{i}
. Let
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
be the translate of
\mathfrak{n}
by the reflection around
\alpha_{i}
in the Weyl group. This is the subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
spanned by
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
for all positive roots
\alpha
except
\alpha_{i}
together with
\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha_{i}}
. The reflection around
\alpha_{i}
gives an isomorphism
\mathfrak{n}\to\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
which is compatible with the grading in the sense that it sends a vector graded by
\beta
to the vector
w_{i}(\beta)
where
w_{i}
is the simple reflection associated to
\alpha_{i}
in the Weyl group acting on the root lattice
\Lambda
. This induces an isomorphism
H^{*}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})\to H^{*}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{1})
which is compatible in the same sense. We will construct another isomorphism
\bigoplus_{j}H^{j}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})\to\bigoplus_{j}H^{j}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{1})
. The composition of these two will give us an “action” of the reflection around
\alpha_{i}
on
\bigoplus_{j}H^{j}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})
. Combining these actions for all
i
gives enough information to calculate
\bigoplus_{j}H^{j}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})
.

To do this, let
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
be the intersection of
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, i.e. the subalgebra spanned by
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
for all positive roots
\alpha
except
\alpha_{i}
. Let
\mathfrak{p}
be the subalgebra generated by
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, i.e. the subalgebra spanned by
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
for all positive roots
\alpha
together with
\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha_{i}}
and
[\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha_{i}},\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha_{i}}]
.

Lemma 6.14
.

With
\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime},\mathfrak{n}_{0},\mathfrak{p}
as above,
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
is an ideal in
\mathfrak{p}
. Furthermore,
\mathfrak{p}/\mathfrak{n}_{0}
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, with
\mathfrak{n}/\mathfrak{n}_{0}
the space generated by the unique positive root
E\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}/\mathfrak{n}_{0}
the space generated by the unique negative root
F\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
.

Proof.

First we check that
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
is an ideal in
\mathfrak{n}
with quotient
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha_{i}}
. This is because
[\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha_{i}},\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha_{i}+\alpha}\subseteq\mathfrak{n}_{0}
for any positive root
\alpha
, since
\alpha
positive and
\alpha_{i}
simple implies
\alpha+\alpha_{i}
positive but
\alpha+\alpha_{i}\neq\alpha_{i}
. Since the roots of
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
are the positive roots for a different linear form on
\Lambda
(lying in an adjacent Weyl chamber) the same argument shows
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
is an ideal in
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, with quotient
\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha_{i}}
.

Since
\mathfrak{p}
is generated by
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, it immediately follows that
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
is an ideal in
\mathfrak{p}
, with quotient generated by
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha_{i}}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha_{i}}
. It is standard that
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha_{i}}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha_{i}}
generate a Lie algebra isomorphic to
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, with
\alpha_{i}
the unique positive root and
-\alpha_{i}
the unique negative root.∎

Lemma 6.15
.

There is an isomorphism between
H^{j}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})
and the hypercohomology
H^{j}_{\langle E\rangle}(H^{*}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}(\mathbf{1}))
and similarly an isomorphism between
H^{j}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{1})
and the hypercohomology
H^{j}_{\langle F\rangle}(H^{*}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}(\mathbf{1}))
. These isomorphisms respect the
\Lambda
-grading.

Proof.

Whenever we have a lie algebra
\mathfrak{a}
with ideal
\mathfrak{b}
, we have an isomorphism

H^{j}_{\mathfrak{a}}(\mathbf{1})\cong H^{j}_{\mathfrak{a}/\mathfrak{b}}(H^{*}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\mathbf{1})).

Applying this isomorphism to
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{n}
or
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{b}
yields, by Lemma
6.14
, the desired isomorphisms. These are natural in the sense that they are compatible with automorphisms of the Lie algebra, which gives the compatibility with grading as the grading arises from a family of automorphisms parameterized by
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{r}
. ∎

Thus we will need to understand how to relate the hypercohomology groups
H^{j}_{\langle E\rangle}(M)
and
H^{j}_{\langle F\rangle}(M)
for a complex of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-representations
M
. Such a complex splits as a sum of representations, but we do not want to take such a splitting immediately. It is more convenient to first write down natural maps in the setting of a general graded complex, then show they are isomorphisms by calculating in the case of a finite-dimensional representation.

We have the hypercohomology spectral sequences

H^{j}_{\langle E\rangle}(\mathcal{H}^{k}(M))\to H^{j+k}_{\langle E\rangle}(M)

and

H^{j}_{\langle F\rangle}(\mathcal{H}^{k}(M)))\to H^{p+q}_{\langle E\rangle}(M).

The Lie algebras
\langle E\rangle
and
\langle F\rangle
, being one-dimensional, have cohomology of any representation concentrated in degrees
0
and
1
, with the cohomology in degree
0
isomorphic to the invariants and the cohomology in degree
1
isomorphic to the coinvariants, so the spectral sequences collapse to short exact sequences

0\to(\mathcal{H}^{k-1}(M))_{E}\to H^{k}_{\langle E\rangle}(M)\to(\mathcal{H}^{k}(M))^{E}\to 0

0\to(\mathcal{H}^{k-1}(M))_{F}\to H^{k}_{\langle F\rangle}(M)\to(\mathcal{H}^{k}(M))^{E}\to 0

where superscripts
E,F
denote invariants and subscripts
E,F
denote coinvariants.

The natural maps from invariants to the underlying vector space and from the underlying vector space to coinvariants give natural maps

H^{k}_{\langle E\rangle}(M)\to(\mathcal{H}^{k}(M))^{E}\to\mathcal{H}^{k}(M)\to(\mathcal{H}^{k}(M))_{F}\to H^{k+1}_{\langle F\rangle}(M)

(6.33)

H^{k}_{\langle F\rangle}(M)\to(\mathcal{H}^{k}(M))^{F}\to\mathcal{H}^{k}(M)\to(\mathcal{H}^{k}(M))_{E}\to H^{k+1}_{\langle E\rangle}(M)

(6.34)

We say a complex of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-modules is
\Lambda
-graded if each module in the context admits a
\Lambda
-grading as a vector space, with the action of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
compatible with the
\Lambda
-grading, where
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
is
\Lambda
-graded so that
E
has grade
\alpha_{i}
and
F
has grade
-\alpha_{i}
.

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
M
be a complex of
\Lambda
-graded
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
modules.

1.

The natural maps
H^{k}_{\langle E\rangle}(M)\to(\mathcal{H}^{k}(M))^{E}
and
H^{k}_{\langle F\rangle}(M)\to(\mathcal{H}^{k}(M))^{E}
respect the
\Lambda
-grading.

2.

The map
(\mathcal{H}^{k-1}(M))_{E}\to H^{k}_{\langle E\rangle}(M)
subtracts
\alpha_{i}
from the grading while
(\mathcal{H}^{k-1}(M))_{F}\to H^{k}_{\langle F\rangle}(M)
adds
\alpha_{i}
to the grading.

Proof.

The cohomology of the one-dimensional Lie algebra
\langle E\rangle
with coefficients in the representation
\mathcal{H}^{k}(M)
can be computed from the Chevally-Eilenberg complex
\mathcal{H}^{k}(M)\to\operatorname{Hom}(\langle E\rangle,\mathcal{H}^{k}(M))
with the map sending
v\in\mathcal{H}^{k}(M)
to the unique linear form that takes value
[E,v]
on
E
. The kernel is isomorphic to the invariants in a way that respects the grading. On the other hand, the isomorphism
M\to\operatorname{Hom}(\langle E\rangle,\mathcal{H}^{k}(M))
that sends
w
to the unique linear form that takes value
w
on
E
shifts the grading by minus the grade of
E
, i.e. by
-\alpha
, so the isomorphism from the coinvariants of
M
to the cokernel also shifts the grade by
-\alpha_{i}
.

Symmetrically, the same isomorphisms in the case of
\langle F\rangle
shift the grade by
0
and
+\alpha_{i}
. ∎

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
M
be a complex of
\Lambda
-graded
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
modules. The map (
6.33
) shifts the grading by
+\alpha_{i}
while the map (
6.34
) shifts the grading by
-\alpha_{i}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.16
, in (
6.33
), the last arrow adds
\alpha_{i}
to the grading while the first three arrows preserve the grading, so the composition adds
\alpha_{i}
to the grading. In (
6.34
), the last arrow subtracts
\alpha_{i}
from the grading while the first three arrows preserve the grading, so the composition subtracts
\alpha_{i}
from the grading. ∎

We use
\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}V
to denote the
\beta
-graded piece of a
\Lambda
-graded vector space
V
. It follows from Lemma
6.17
that we have natural maps

H^{k}_{\langle E\rangle}(M)[\beta]\to H^{k+1}_{\langle F\rangle}(M)[\beta+\alpha_{i}]

(6.35)

H^{k}_{\langle F\rangle}(M)[\beta+\alpha_{i}]\to H^{k+1}_{\langle E\rangle}(M)[\beta]

(6.36)

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
M
be a bounded complex of
\Lambda
-graded
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-modules with finite-dimensional cohomology objects. Assume the eigenvalue of
H\in\mathfrak{s}l_{2}
on
\mathcal{H}^{i}(M)[\beta]
is equal to
2\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle/\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle

For each
\beta
, (
6.35
) is an isomorphism for
\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle>-\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle
and (
6.36
) is an isomorphism for
\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle<0
.

Proof.

Since
M
is a sum of shifts of irreducible representations, it suffices to prove this for
M
a shift of an irreducible representation, and we we may as well assume the shift is
0
. The
k+1
-dimensional irreducible representation
V_{k}
is generated by a vector
v
such that
F\cdot v=0
, and has a basis
v,E\cdot v,\dots,E^{k}\cdot v
. Furthermore
v
is an eigenvector of
H
with eigenvalue
-k
, and so the grade
\beta_{0}
of
v
satisfies
\langle\beta_{0},\alpha_{i}\rangle=-k\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle/2
.

The
E
-invariants are generated by
E^{k}v
and the
E
-coinvariants by
v
. Since
H^{0}_{\langle E\rangle}
is the
E
-invariants and
H^{1}_{\langle E\rangle}
is the
E
-coinvariants shifted in grade by
\alpha_{i}
(Lemma
6.16
), we have
H^{0}_{\langle E\rangle}(V_{k})=\mathbb{C}
in grade
\beta_{0}+k\alpha_{i}
and
H^{1}_{\langle E\rangle}(V_{k})=\mathbb{C}
in grade
\beta_{0}-\alpha_{i}
Similarly, the
F
-invariants are generated by
v
and the
F
-coinvariants by
E^{k}v
, so
H^{0}_{\langle F\rangle}(V_{k})=\mathbb{C}
in grade
\beta_{0}
and
H^{1}_{\langle F\rangle}(V_{k})=\mathbb{C}
in grade
\beta_{0}+(k+1)\alpha_{i}
.

The map
H^{k}_{\langle E\rangle}(V_{k})[\beta]\to H^{k+1}_{\langle F\rangle}(V_{k})[\beta+\alpha_{i}]
is trivially an isomorphism whenever both sides are
0
. This holds unless
\beta=\beta_{0}+k\alpha_{i},k=0
or
\beta=\beta_{0}-\alpha_{i},k=1
or
\beta=\beta_{0}-\alpha_{i},k=-1
. The second two cases have

\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle=-(k+2)\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle/2\leq-\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle

so these can be ignored as they do not satisfy our hypothesis. In the first case, both sides of the map are one-dimensional, generated by
E^{k}v
. Since the map is defined by taking a cohomology class, viewing as an invariant, then as an element, then as a coinvariant, and then as another class, it sends the class represented by
E^{k}v
to the class represented by
E^{k}v
and hence is an isomorphism.

Similarly,
H^{k}_{\langle F\rangle}(V_{k})([\beta+\alpha_{i}]\to H^{k+1}_{\langle E\rangle}(V_{k})[\beta]
is trivially an isomorphism whenever both sides are zero. This holds unless
\beta=\beta_{0}+k\alpha_{i},k=-1
or
\beta=\beta_{0}-\alpha_{i},k=0
or
\beta=\beta_{0}+k\alpha_{i},k=1
. The first and last cases have

\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle=k\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle/2\geq 0

so these can be ignored as they do not satisfy our hypothesis. In the middle case, both sides are one-dimensional, generated by
v
. Again, this forces the map to be an isomorphism in the remaining case.∎

Corollary 6.19
.

The natural map

\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})\to\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}H^{k+1}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{1})

is an isomorphism for
\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle>-\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle
and the natural map

\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{1})\to\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}H^{k+1}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})

is an isomorphism for
\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle<0
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemmas
6.15
and
6.18
, taking
M=H^{*}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}(\mathbf{1})
once we check that
H^{*}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}(\mathbf{1})
satisfies the compatibilities. The compatibility between the
\Lambda
-grading of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
and the
\Lambda
-grading of
H^{*}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}(\mathbf{1})
is clear because
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
are both
\Lambda
-graded subspaces of
\mathfrak{g}
and thus the action of
\mathfrak{p}/\mathfrak{n}_{0}\cong\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
on the
\Lambda
-graded Chevalley-Eilenberg complex computing
H^{*}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}(\mathbf{1})
is compatible with the grading.

For the compatibility between the grading and the action of
H\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
, we first check the same compatibility for the action of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
on
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
, where it follows from the definition of the weight space grading on the Lie algebra. Then the same compatibility will hold for the Chevalley-Eilenberg complex and thus its cohomology. ∎

Proof of Proposition
6.13
.

The isomorphism
\mathfrak{n}\to\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
gives an isomorphism between
\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{1})
and
\operatorname{Gr}^{w_{i}(\beta+\alpha_{i})}H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})
and we have
w_{i}(\beta+\alpha_{i})=w_{i}(\beta)-\alpha_{i}
. Composing this isomorphism with the first isomorphism of Corollary
6.19
gives the first isomorphism above, and composing with the second isomorphism of Corollary
6.19
and reversing the order gives the second isomorphism above.

When both isomorphisms hold, their composition implies an isomorphism
\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})\cong\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}H^{k+2}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})
which since Ext groups are concentrated in nonnegative degrees implies by induction that
\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})=0
for all
k
. ∎

Lemma 6.20
.

We have
\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})=0
unless
\beta
is a negative integer combination of exactly
k
positive roots.

Proof.

This follows from the description of the Chevalley-Eilenberg complex, which writes
H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})
as a subquotient of
\wedge^{k}(\mathfrak{n})^{\vee}
, since
\mathfrak{n}
is spanned by the weight spaces of the positive roots so that
\wedge^{k}(\mathfrak{n})
is spanned by wedge products of weight spaces of
k
positive roots. ∎

Lemma 6.21
.

Let
w_{i_{1}}\dots w_{i_{n}}
be a reduced word in a Weyl group. Let
\gamma
be a vector in the positive Weyl chamber. Then
\langle w_{i_{1}}\dots w_{i_{n}}\gamma,\alpha_{i_{1}}\rangle\leq 0
.

Proof.

Suppose for contradiction that
\langle w_{i_{1}}\dots w_{i_{n}}\gamma,\alpha_{i_{1}}\rangle>0
. Then

\langle\gamma,w_{i_{n}}\dots w_{i_{2}}\alpha_{i_{1}}\rangle=-\langle\gamma,w_{i_{n}}\dots w_{i_{1}}\alpha_{i_{1}}\rangle=-\langle w_{i_{1}}\dots w_{i_{n}}\gamma,\alpha_{i_{1}}\rangle<0

so
w_{i_{n}}\dots w_{i_{2}}\alpha_{i_{1}}
is a negative root. Since
\alpha_{i}
is a positive root, there must be some
j\geq 1
such that
w_{i_{j}}\dots w_{i_{2}}\alpha_{i_{1}}
is a positive root but
w_{i_{j+1}}\dots w_{i_{2}}\alpha_{i_{1}}
is a negative root. Since each reflection through a simple root moves the positive Weyl chamber to an adjacent Weyl chamber and thus changes only one positive root, i.e. that simple root, to a negative root, we must have
w_{i_{j}}\dots w_{i_{2}}\alpha_{i_{1}}=\alpha_{i_{j+1}}
so

w_{i_{j+1}}=w_{i_{j}}\dots w_{i_{2}}w_{i_{1}}w_{i_{2}}^{-1}\dots w_{i_{j}}^{-1}=w_{i_{j}}^{-1}\dots w_{i_{2}}^{-1}w_{i_{1}}w_{i_{2}}\dots w_{i_{j}}

(since each reflection is its own inverse) which implies

w_{i_{1}}\dots w_{i_{n}}=w_{i_{2}}\dots w_{i_{j}}w_{i_{j+1}}w_{i_{j+1}}w_{i_{j+2}}\dots w_{i_{n}}=w_{i_{2}}\dots w_{i_{j}}w_{i_{j+2}}\dots w_{i_{n}},

contradicting the assumption that
w_{i_{1}}\dots w_{i_{n}}
is reduced. ∎

Proof of Theorem
6.12
.

Fix any
\beta
, and let
w
be an element of the Weyl group such that
\langle w^{-1}(\beta+\rho),\alpha_{i}\rangle\geq 0
for all
i
. This is possible since the set of vectors satisfying these conditions form the positive Weyl chamber, and we can choose an element of the Weyl group that sends any vector into the positive Weyl chamber. Equivalently, we have
\langle w^{-1}(\beta+\rho)-\rho,\alpha_{i}\rangle\geq-\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle/2

It suffices to prove that
\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})=0
unless
\beta=w(\rho)-\rho
for
\rho
half the sum of the positive roots and
k=\operatorname{length}(w)
for this value of
w
, and is
1
-dimensional in that case. Indeed, if the condition holds for this value of
w
then it clearly holds for some value of
w
, and if it holds for some value of
w
then
w
is the unique
w^{\prime}
such that
\langle w^{\prime-1}(\beta+\rho),\alpha_{i}\rangle\geq 0
, since
\beta+\rho=w(\rho)-\rho+\rho=w(\rho)
, and
w(\rho)
lies in the interior of its Weyl chamber so there is a unique element
w^{\prime}=w
sending it to the positive Weyl chamber.

We prove this claim by induction on
\operatorname{length}(w)
.

For the base case where
w
is the identity element, we assume
\langle\beta+\rho,\alpha_{i}\rangle\geq 0
for all
i
, which implies
\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle\geq-\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle/2
for all
i
. By Lemma
6.20
if
\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})\neq 0
then
\beta
is a negative combination of exactly
k
roots. If
k=0
it follows that
\beta=0=w(\rho)-\rho
for
w
the identity element, and in this case
H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})[\beta]=\mathbf{1}
is the
\mathfrak{n}
-invariants of
\mathbf{1}
. If
k>0
then it follows that
\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle<0
for some
i
since otherwise
\beta
would lie in the positive cone and hence could not be a nontrivial combination of negative roots, and thus

-\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle<-\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle/2\leq\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle<0

so
H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})[\beta]=0
by Proposition
6.13
.

For the induction step, note that we can write
w=w_{i}w^{\prime}
for some
i
and some
w^{\prime}
in the Weyl group where
\operatorname{length}(w^{\prime})=\operatorname{length}(w)-1
. Let

\beta^{\prime}=w_{i}^{-1}(\beta+\rho)-\rho=w_{i}(\beta+\rho)-\rho=w_{i}(\beta)+w_{i}(\rho)-\rho=w_{i}(\beta)-\alpha_{i}

Observe that, by the induction hypothesis applied to
\beta^{\prime},w^{\prime}
, since

w^{{}^{\prime}-1}(\beta^{\prime}+\rho)=w^{{}^{\prime}-1}(w_{i}^{-1}(\beta+\rho))=w^{-1}(\beta+\rho)

is in the positive Weyl chamber, the group
\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta^{\prime}}H^{k-1}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})
vanishes unless
\beta^{\prime}=w^{\prime}(\rho)-\rho
and
k-1=\operatorname{length}(w^{\prime})=\operatorname{length}(w)-1
, which is equivalent to

\beta=w_{i}(\beta^{\prime}+\rho)-\rho=w_{i}(w^{\prime}(\rho))-\rho=w(\rho)-\rho

and
k=\operatorname{length}(w)
, and is one-dimensional in that case. So it suffices to check

\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}H^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})=\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta^{\prime}}H^{k-1}_{\mathfrak{n}}(\mathbf{1})

which follows from Proposition
6.13
as long as
\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle<0
.

By Lemma
6.21
applied to
\gamma=w^{-1}(\beta+\rho)
in the positive Weyl chamber and any reduced word for
w
beginning with
w_{i}
, we must have
\langle\beta+\rho,\alpha_{i}\rangle\leq 0
, which since
\langle\rho,\alpha_{i}\rangle>0
implies
\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle<0
. ∎

6.6
Beyond the toy model

In this subsection, we use the notation
M\{\alpha\}
for a
\Lambda
-graded module
M
to denote the module
M
with the grading shifted by
\alpha
, i.e. each homogenous element of grade
\beta
in
M
becomes a homogeneous element of grade
\beta+\alpha
in
M\{\alpha\}
.

One could try to generalize the proof of Proposition
6.13
to the setting of quantum groups. Much of the argument transfers, but there is one crucial obstruction that we now explain.

Here,
\mathfrak{n}
is replaced by the positive part of Lusztig’s small quantum group
u_{v}(\mathfrak{g})
. We replace the Lie algebra cohomology with the Ext group of the trivial representation with itself over this algebra. (Since the Lie algebra cohomology of the trivial representation is simply the Ext group of the trivial representation with itself, this is a direct generalization.)
We replace the Weyl group with the braid group, so
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
is replaced by the image of
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
under the action of the
i
th standard generator of the braid group under Lusztig’s braid group action. We can replace
\mathfrak{p}
with the subalgebra generated by
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, and
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
by the intersection of these subalgebras. Lemma
6.14
could be replaced by short exact sequences relating these Hopf algebras to
u_{v}(\mathfrak{s}l_{2})
and its positive and negative parts. The spectral sequences could be replaced by the spectral sequences associated to the short exact sequences of Hopf algebras.

A suitable analogue of Lemma
6.18
would be the following statement
6.22
, which unfortunately is false.

Let
\Lambda
be a finitely generated abelian group (which we will take to be a root lattice) and
\alpha_{i}\in\Lambda
an element (which we will take to be a root). Let
u_{v}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
be Lusztig’s small quantum group, endowed with a
\Lambda
-grading where
E
has grade
\alpha_{i}
,
F
has grade
-\alpha_{i}
, and
K
has grade
0
. We say a complex of
u_{v}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
models is
\Lambda
-graded if it is endowed with a
\Lambda
-grading as a complex of vector spaces for which the action of
u_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
is compatible with the grading.

Non-theorem 6.22
.

Let
{v}
be a root of unity whose square has order
n_{i}
. Let
M
be a complex of
\Lambda
-graded
u_{v}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
-modules with finite-dimensional cohomology objects. Assume the eigenvalue of
K\in\mathfrak{u}_{v}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
on
\mathcal{H}^{i}(M)[\beta]
is equal to
{v}^{2\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle/\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle}
. Let
\langle E\rangle_{v}
and
\langle F\rangle_{v}
be the subalgebras of
u_{v}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
generated by
E
and
F
respectively. Fix
\beta\in\Lambda
and let
c\in\{1,\dots,n_{i}\}
be congruent to
\frac{2\langle\beta,\alpha_{i}\rangle}{\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle}+1
modulo
n_{i}
. Fix
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. If
c\neq n_{i}
then we have

\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}\operatorname{Ext}^{j}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)-\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+(n-c)\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{j-1}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)

=\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}-c\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{j}_{\langle F\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)-\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+(1-n_{i})\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{j-1}_{\langle F\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)

and if
c=n_{i}
we have

\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}\operatorname{Ext}^{j}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)[\beta]=\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+(1-n_{i})\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{j}_{\langle F\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M).

A counterexample is provided by the following:

Example 6.23
.

We consider the special case when
M
is a module, viewed as a complex by placing in degree
0
, and
j=0
. Since cohomology groups in negative degree vanish, the degree
0
cohomology with respect to
E
are the
E
-invariants, and the same is true for
F
, the putative equation becomes

\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}M^{E}=\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}-c\alpha_{i}}M^{F}

The map
\beta\mapsto\beta+\alpha_{i}-c_{\beta}\alpha_{i}
is a bijection, with inverse
\beta\mapsto\beta+(n_{i}-c_{\beta}-1)\alpha_{i}
so summing over
\beta
we obtain
\dim M^{E}=\dim M^{F}
.

But this is absurd. A Verma module will always have a one-dimensional space of invariants for
F
but typically a two-dimensional space of invariants for
E
.

Explicitly, Let
v
be a root of unity of odd order
n_{i}
. Then
u_{v}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
is the algebra with generators
E,F,K,K^{-1}
and relations
EF-FE=\frac{K-K^{-1}}{v-v^{-}1},KE=v^{2}EK,KF=v^{-2}FK,E^{n_{i}}=0,F^{n_{i}}=0,K^{2n_{i}}=1
, as well as
KK^{-1}=K^{-1}K=1
.

The Verma module
M
can be presented as the module freely generated by a vector
x
with relations
Ex=0
and
Kx=v^{s}x
for some integer
s\in\{0,\dots,n_{i}-2\}
.

Even more explicitly, this is the module with basis
x,Fx,\dots,F^{n_{i}-1}x
where

F\cdot F^{t}x=F^{t+1}x\textrm{ for }t<n_{i}-1

F\cdot F^{n_{i}-1}x=0,

K\cdot F^{t}x=v^{s-2t}F^{t}x,

EF^{t}x=\frac{v^{s+1}+v^{-1-s}-v^{s+1-2t}-v^{2t-s-1}}{(v-v^{-1})^{2}}F^{t-1}x\textrm{ for }t>0

Fx=0.

One can verify directly that the relations are satisfied for this module.

We give the generator
x
a grade
\beta_{0}\in\Lambda
satisfying
\langle\beta_{0},\alpha_{i}\rangle=\frac{s}{2}\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle
. Then
M
is a complex of
\Lambda
-graded
u_{v}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
-modules with finite-dimensional cohomology objects and satisfies the compatibility with the eigenvalue of
K
.

The space of
F
-invariants is generated by
F^{n_{i}-1}x
. On the other hand, we have
EF^{t}=0
if and only if
v^{s+1}+v^{-1-s}-v^{s+1-2t}-v^{2t-s-1}=0
which occurs if and only if
t\equiv 0\bmod n_{i}
or
i\equiv s+1\bmod n_{i}
, since
v
has odd order so that
v^{s+1}
and
v^{-1-s}
can’t cancel so that
v^{s+1}
must cancel one of the other two terms. Since
s\in\{0,\dots,n_{i}-2\}
this occurs exactly when
t=0
or
t=s+1
, so that
M
has a two-dimensional space of
E
-invariants, giving the desired contradiction.

Therefore it seems one cannot prove the functional equation based solely on the hypercohomology description, without further information on the cohomology of
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
.

One claim that can be checked directly is a functional equation for Euler characteristics.

Lemma 6.24
.

Let
v
be a root of unity whose square has order
n_{i}>1
. Let
M
be
\Lambda
-graded complex of
u_{v}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
-modules such that for each
\beta\in u_{v}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
, the
\beta
-graded part of the cohomology
\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}\mathcal{H}^{k}(M)
is finite-dimensional for all
k
and zero for all but finitely many
k
. Then

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}\operatorname{Ext}^{k}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+n_{i}\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{k}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)

=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}\mathcal{H}^{k}(M)-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}\mathcal{H}^{k}(M)

Proof.

The key player will be the complex
U
of
\langle E\rangle_{v}
-modules defined by
U^{0}=\langle E\rangle_{v}
,
U^{1}=\langle E\rangle_{v}\{-\alpha_{i}\}
,
U^{k}=0
for
k\neq 0,1
and where
d^{0}\colon U^{0}\to U^{1}
is multiplication by
E
. This is a
\Lambda
-graded complex of
\langle E\rangle_{v}
-modules, so
\operatorname{RHom}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(U,M)
carries a
\Lambda
-grading. We calculate

\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{RHom}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(U,M)

in two ways. On the one hand, by the spectral sequence associated to the filtration with associated graded pieces
U^{0}
and
U^{1}
we have

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{RHom}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(U,M)

=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{RHom}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\langle E\rangle_{v},M)-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{RHom}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\langle E\rangle_{v})\{-\alpha_{i}\},M)

=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}\mathcal{H}^{k}(M)-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}\mathcal{H}^{k}(M\{\alpha_{i}\})

=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}\mathcal{H}^{k}(M)-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}\mathcal{H}^{k}(M).

On the other hand, by the spectral sequence associated to the filtration with associated graded pieces
\ker d^{0}
and
\operatorname{coker}d^{0}
we have

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{RHom}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(U,M)

=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{RHom}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\ker d^{0},M)-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{RHom}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\operatorname{coker}d^{0},M)

Now
\operatorname{coker}d^{0}=\langle E\rangle_{v}/E\langle E\rangle_{v}\{-\alpha_{i}\}=\mathbf{1}\{-\alpha_{i}\}
and thus

\operatorname{RHom}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\operatorname{coker}d^{0},M)=\operatorname{RHom}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)\{\alpha_{i}\}

so that

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{RHom}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\operatorname{coker}d^{0},M)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}\operatorname{RHom}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)

Since
\langle E\rangle_{v}=\mathbb{C}[E]/E^{n_{i}}
, the kernel
\ker d^{0}
of multiplication by
E
is generated by
E^{n-1}
and thus is isomorphic to
\mathbf{1}\{(n_{i}-1)\alpha_{i}\}
so that

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{RHom}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\ker d^{0},M)[\beta+\alpha_{i}]=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+n_{i}\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{RHom}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M).

Combining the equations, and subtracting, gives the formula. ∎

Lemma 6.25
.

Let
v
be a root of unity whose square has order
n_{i}>1
. Let
M
be
\Lambda
-graded complex of
u_{v}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
-modules such that for each
\beta\in u_{v}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
, the
\beta
-graded part of the cohomology
\mathcal{H}^{k}(M)[\beta]
is finite-dimensional for all
k
and zero for all but finitely many
k
. Then

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}\operatorname{Ext}^{k}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+n_{i}\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{k}_{\langle E\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)

=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+(1-n_{i})\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{k}_{\langle F\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{k}_{\langle F\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)

Proof.

The algebra
u_{v}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
is symmetric under exchanging
E
and
F
and
K
and
K^{-1}
. This has the effect of negating the grading. Thus, we may exchange
E
and
F
in Lemma
6.24
, also exchanging
\alpha_{i}
and
-\alpha_{i}
, to obtain

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}\operatorname{Ext}^{k}_{\langle F\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta-n_{i}\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{k}_{\langle F\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)

=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}\mathcal{H}^{k}(M)-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta-\alpha_{i}}\mathcal{H}^{k}(M)

and shift by
\alpha_{i}
to get

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{k}_{\langle F\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+(1-n_{i})\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{Ext}^{k}_{\langle F\rangle_{v}}(\mathbf{1},M)

=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta+\alpha_{i}}\mathcal{H}^{k}(M)-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{k}\dim\operatorname{Gr}^{\beta}\mathcal{H}^{k}(M)

Negating, and combining with Lemma
6.24
, we get the stated equation.∎

It should be possible to use Lemma
6.25
to prove Euler characteristic analogues of Corollary
6.19
and Proposition
6.13
, and prove the Euler characteristic variant of the functional equation.

Even more generally than quantum groups, one could work with Nichols algebras of diagonal type. Here the Weyl group would be replaced by the Weyl groupoid defined by Heckenberger. In the notation of
[
34
, p. 180]
, we replace
\mathfrak{n}
by a Nichols algebra
\mathcal{B}(V)
of diagonal type, replace
\mathfrak{p}
by
(\mathcal{B}(V)^{\mathrm{op}}\#H_{i}^{\mathrm{cop}})^{\mathrm{op}}
, replace
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
by the subalgebra
\mathcal{B}_{i}
(which is another Nichols algebra of diagonal type, associated to a possibly-different vector space), and replace
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
by
\operatorname{ker}y_{i}^{L}
. It seems likely that these Hopf algebras lie in exact sequences relating them to
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(q_{ii})
and its positive and negative parts, in which case one can apply the spectral sequences associated to a short exact sequence of Hopf algebras.

Again, Lemma
6.25
should be applicable here, and give analogues of Corollary
6.19
and Proposition
6.13
.
